#232. Children’s Place files for bankruptcy protection, seeks buyer

Children's clothing retailer The Children's Place has filed for chapter 11 bankruptcy protection, it said Sunday.

The company is looking to sell substantially all of its assets and is seeking offers from potential buyers by Dec. 9, with a goal to close a deal by the end of December.

The retailer, whose brands include The Children's Place and Gymboree, says it operates two digital storefronts and 514 North American stores, and has distribution in 13 countries through franchise and wholesale partners. The company's performance has taken a hit in recent years from tariff hikes and slower consumer spending, among other factors.

Its stores and e-commerce sites remain open for now, and the company is planning to continue its ongoing inventory sales with court approval.

"For nearly 60 years, The Children's Place has supported growing families with apparel that customers have come to know and love. We recognize the significance of this development for our employees, customers, vendors, and other partners, and we are committed to conducting an efficient and transparent sale process," said Rudy Morando, co-chief restructuring officer of The Children's Place. "In the meantime, we remain focused on serving the families who have trusted The Children's Place for generations."

This move comes days after interim Children's Place CEO Muhammad Asif Seemab and Chief Strategy Officer Muhammad Umair stepped down from their roles. Seemab and Umair will both remain on the company's board. CFO John Szczepanski took on the role of acting president and principal executive officer.

Seemab is also managing director of Saudi-based Mithaq Capital, which took a majority stake in The Children's Place in 2024 when the company was facing financial trouble.

Even with support from the investors, the company has not been able to significantly improve its financial outlook. In its bankruptcy filing, the company reported about $742 million in assets, compared to about $881 million in debts.

In the second fiscal quarter of 2026, its most recently reported quarter, the company's net sales dropped nearly 19% from the same period the year prior. At the time, Seemab said in a news release that during the quarter the company was "stabilizing our customer file" and "driving traffic to our stores and websites," and saw "lower conversion, combined with a very clearance-heavy mix."

He also pointed to tough comparisons to the prior-year period, due in part to back-to-school shopping demand that "was uniquely elevated ... ahead of anticipated tariff price increases."

The Children's Place stock has plummeted in recent years and was trading around 18 cents per share on Monday.

#230. 'An annoying buzz': The search for stealthier drones

Amazon Prime Air is expanding to 500 US towns and cities

Nature lover Ashley Schmidt no longer spends much time in her garden, in the Dallas suburb of North Garland.

She's also given up on cycling to the local parks where she previously enjoyed taking close-up photos of the bugs, lizards and toads.

It's all because she can't stand the sound of the delivery drones that fly overhead, depositing her neighbours' online orders.

"They emit this very annoying buzz. It triggers my fight or flight response," says the 44-year-old administrative assistant.

"You can hear the drones on approach and you can hear them inside your home when they pass directly overhead and then you hear them as they leave," she says.

Amazon says it is working to reduce the noise of its drone fleet

Schmidt is one of a growing number of people living with the noise of drones dispatched by companies including Amazon and Walmart as they seek to speed up home deliveries.

Delivery drone services, not long ago a novelty, are becoming part of everyday life for many people in the US.

Among them, Amazon Prime Air is in the process of expanding deliveries to nearly 500 cities and towns, delivering items that fit into a space the size of a shoe box. It has also launched to customers in and around Darlington in the north of England.

Smaller drone delivery services are popping up too: in some US cities, customers can order flying treats from their local pizza joint or ice cream parlour.

Even in cities where noise is already a steady part of life, the sound of drones seems to irk in a particular way.

While the steady passing of traffic might blend into the background, the sudden whine of a drone's rotors can pierce the everyday hum.

The sound tends to be "particularly noticeable, even when overall sound levels are relatively low," says Antonio Torija Martinez, professor in acoustic engineering and psychoacoustics at the University of Salford.

Unlike planes, drones operate at low altitudes and may fly directly over residential areas.

"They can expose communities that have not previously experienced significant aircraft noise, and who may therefore be more sensitive to changes in their local soundscape," says Torija Martinez.

He points to research showing that people respond strongly to a noise when they consider the source to be "novel, intrusive or frequent". People who benefit from a drone delivery service, for example, may be less bothered by the noise.

However, complaints about the sound of drones are likely to grow, he warns: "At the moment we are seeing some of these problems localised in some areas, butâ¦ as the drone sector expands, more and more of these complaints will happen."

In the US, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) regulates commercial drones, but it has come under fire for allowing noisy drone delivery operations. The FAA says operators and local community leaders have primary responsibility for addressing any community concerns.

Greenjets encloses some of the blades on its drone inside a pod

Might better technology muffle the irritating buzz?

One startup, Greenjets, set out to tackle both "noise level and the frequency of that noise," as Darren James, future technologies lead at the company, puts it.

The London-based business has designed a propulsion system it claims can reduce drone noise by at least two thirds.

While standard drones use spinning propellers that slice through open air, creating that high-pitched buzz, Greenjets encloses spinning blades inside a pod, to muffle the sound and direct the noise away from the ground.

Its system also quietens things down through slower rotor speed and by using materials that reduce vibration noise.

Making quieter drones isn't just about keeping the noise down for neighbours who are already sick of airborne deliveries.

Greenjets' low-noise tech has been used by police and harbour security teams in Germany. It has also helped conservationists in Africa, who are seeking to track poachers from above without being detected.

Battlefield drone operators also have good reason to keep the noise down, to avoid alerting the enemy of the approach of their unmanned vehicles.

"There are some efforts to reduce the sound of drones. The US Special Forces are interested, at an experimental stage," says Robert Tollast, a research fellow at Whitehall think tank The Royal United Services Institute (RUSI).

But he stresses that drone speed, rather than stealthiness, tends to come first for larger drones used in the war between Russia and Ukraine. "Make it faster, not less quiet, seems to be the approach," says Tollast.

Sharrow Engineering developed twisted propellers that reduce noise

For Detroit-based entrepreneur, Greg Sharrow, it was the challenge of broadcasting a classical music performance back in 2012 that set him on the path to quieter drone design.

He needed to film a violinist from a tricky angle and a drone seemed the only solution â except that it would be far too noisy.

As a starting point, "I knew that most of the noise from the drone was coming from the propeller, not the motor," he says. Specifically, it was the tips of the blades that he realised were creating the noise as they swiped through the air.

"I asked myself a very simple question, what would a tipless propeller look like and how would it function?"

The result was to create a propeller with twisted loop-shaped blades with no points.

It turned out it was easier to test his propeller in water than it was to repeatedly fly a drone, which led Sharrow to initially develop a less noisy boat propeller. Now, however, his company, Sharrow Engineering, is focusing on developing "ultra-quiet" drone rotors.

"We have validated that the technology works in drone applications and that it is dramatically quieter," says Sharrow.

Companies at the forefront of drone deliveries say they are also working to reduce the noise of their fleets. A spokesperson for Amazon says it has made "measurable sound improvements" to its drones and its latest designs use custom-designed propellers "that produce almost half the perceived sound of the prior generation, with a milder tone".

Drone maker Wing, which provides its vehicles for Walmart deliveries, says its propellers are designed to minimise noise. It also varies its flight paths to prevent repeated overflights over any single home or neighbourhood.

For Schmidt, fed up with the noise of drones, the only truly satisfactory solution would be to halt drone deliveries in her area. "I would like them to completely stop until they figure out how their services stop being a nuisance," she says.

#228. 'I live with a ghost' - inside the haunted homes attracting bargain hunters

The previous occupant of Ryua's apartment, pictured here, sadly took their own life

From the moment Ryua first walked into her rental apartment in Tokyo she believed it to be haunted.

"When I visited the place for a viewing there was a man standing there, glaring at me," she says.

"Since the estate agent couldn't see him, I realised he must be a ghost."

While someone more squeamish would probably have quickly left the flat never to return, Ryua chose to go ahead and move in.

"I'm a bit of a contrarian, so I decided I wanted to become friends with this glaring male ghost," adds Ryua, who works as an aidoru, a young entertainer, and who doesn't wish to share her surname.

Four years later, and still in the same apartment, she says she continues to experience paranormal activity on a daily basis. She says she hears endless footsteps and the ghost talking.

Ryua says that she shares her Tokyo apartment with a ghost

Although Japan has increasingly marked Halloween, external in recent years, many Japanese people take ghosts seriously all year round. The country has a word, "yuurei", which means a lingering, troubled spirit.

These are the ghosts of people who died in violent or tragic circumstances - those who were murdered, killed themselves, or simply were lonely and died alone. If they died at home, then the belief is that their yuurei won't go away.

For house hunters who are not superstitious or squeamish it means there are big bargains to be found. There is a whole category of dwellings called jiko bukken, which means "stigmatised" or "incident" properties.

Under guidelines issued by the government in 2021, Japanese estate agents must disclose suicide, murder, death by fire, and any death where the body was not discovered for an extended period of time.

For rental properties this information must be disclosed for three years, and it must be revealed indefinitely for any property offered for sale.

By contrast, in the UK there is no legal requirement to reveal that a person died in a property. And in the US, it differs from state to state., external For example, in California you have to reveal if a person has died in the property in the past three years, while in Arizona and Oregon you do not.

Ryua was told that a man had killed himself in her apartment. It meant that she gets an almost 30% discount on her rent on her tiny, 25 sq m bedsit in the fashionable ward of Setagaya. She says she pays 45,000 yen ($285; Â£213) a month, instead of what should have been 62,000 yen.

While a house where there had been a murder or suicide would probably be harder to sell or rent out anywhere in the world, Tokyo estate agent Erik Oskamp says that Japanese people are particularly superstitious and averse to stigmatised properties.

"[Even] an apartment next to a cemetery will be difficult to rent out, especially if you can see the tombs from the kitchen," explains Oskamp. "The worst thing is if a suicide has happened in the apartment. Even if somebody has jumped from the roof, many renters will move out. Other issues are a serious crime or a lonely death."

The latter issue, that of a person dying alone, would not generally affect the price of a property in, say, the UK or the US.

But in rural Kagoshima, in Japan's southwest, it enabled Diane Tincher to buy a large three-bedroom house for about half of what would have otherwise been the asking price.

The 69-year-old American has lived in Japan for nearly 40 years, working as a tour guide. She says that the estate agent had been straight with her â there was a heavy discount as an elderly lady had died alone, thereby stigmatising the property.

Tincher paid 20m yen for the house, in a location where similar properties usually sell for up to 45m yen.

"I don't believe in ghosts. But I know that the Japanese are very superstitious," she says. "If there is a ghost I'm thankful to her every day... to live in such a splendid and affordable home."

Diane Tincher says she got a 50% reduction on her home in rural Japan

Researching the house, Tincher had found it on a website called Oshimaland, which lists jiko bukken properties, giving details of the events that occurred. It was launched in 2005 covering Tokyo, before expanding across Japan and then internationally in 2011.

It is crowdsourced, meaning that members of the public add the homes and their details.

Diane Tincher got a huge discount on her home in rural Japan

Teru Oshima, the founder of Oshimaland, says that in the past such properties were "not very attractive options" in Japan.

Yet as house prices have surged in the country in recent years, as well as a big increase in the wider cost of living, one 2025 survey found that more than half of Japanese people would now rent a jiko bukken if it was cheap., external

Back in Setagaya, Ryua says she is happy living with her ghostly flatmate. "I believe that ghosts were once living human beings, so they deserve our respect," she says.

#225. Trump says ‘we don’t need a deal with Iran’

The U.S. should no longer seek a deal with Iran, President Donald Trump said Thursday as he directed Vice President JD Vance to instead focus his attention on corruption and crime in Democratic cities and states.

"We don't need a deal with Iran. Those days have long since passed," Trump wrote in a Truth Social post.

"We've got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we've got their Strait!" Trump wrote, referring to the Strait of Hormuz, a key choke point in the ongoing U.S.-Iran war.

The announcement came just over a week after Republicans suffered huge losses in the midterm election. Vance and other Republicans have pinned those losses on the Iran war, which the U.S. and Israel started in late February.

In the weeks leading up to Election Day, Trump repeatedly suggested the U.S. could resume launching military strikes, which have largely subsided as a tense stalemate has taken hold in the region.

The Wall Street Journal on Wednesday reported that Vance, who has led diplomatic efforts with Iran in prior months, was considering face-to-face talks with a top Iranian security official.

Stock futures declined following Trump's post.

The unpopular Iran war, which Trump and others in his administration said would be won in a matter of weeks, is now in its ninth month with no clear end in sight. The conflict has driven up global energy prices, creating a ripple effect through the economy and exacerbating concerns about inflation and affordability that were paramount to voters in the midterms.

Corruption emerged as another top issue that broke in Democrats' favor in the election. Trump's post Thursday morning attempts to flip the script, blaming Democratic-run areas for allowing corruption and crime to proliferate.

"I have asked Vice President Vance to focus his complete and total attention on the Massive Corruption and Crime taking place in Democrat run Cities and States throughout the U.S.," Trump wrote.

"The Blue States, such as Minnesota, California, Illinois, and many more, are all poorly run, corrupt disasters. Billions of Dollars is being stolen each and every month," the president wrote. "Our Budget could be quickly BALANCED with the correction of this large scale criminal activity."

"CORRUPTION is in the Democrat Leadership's blood!" he added.

#222. How video game remakes tap the power of nostalgia

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is often rated as one of the greatest video games

The moment she saw footage from the remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, Kacey Doran picked up her phone. She had to tell her sister.

"Have you seen this?" messaged Doran, excitedly.

Nearly 30 years ago, a 10-year-old Doran had sat in front of her family's TV, reading out the dialogue on screen to her sister, a year younger, as they inched their way through the original Nintendo 64 game's sprawling, puzzle-filled dungeons. Ocarina's fantasy-like story, and beautiful music, enthralled them both.

It was also the first game Doran ever completed. Now, countless memories rushed back to her as she devoured clips of the fully redesigned version, due to arrive on the Switch 2 later this year.

Doran's sister messaged back: "I'm not tearing upâ¦ YOU'RE tearing up."

News of a video game remake bought back happy memories for Kacey Doran

This is the power of nostalgia â and a perfect example of how video games can trigger it. Doran now teaches an esports course at Rowan University in New Jersey. She says the original Ocarina of Time, released in 1998, had a "magical" quality that she hopes to rediscover, and also share with her sister's children.

The video games industry has long leaned on remakes. From Mario Kart to the Final Fantasy series, or even Pac-Man, some games just keep coming back in updated forms, as new generations of gamers emerge and as computer hardware improves, enabling more detailed graphics and increasingly expansive game worlds.

While the emotional hook may be the most significant draw, putting out a remake can be a double-edged sword. Some gamers are very protective of their favourite titles and new versions don't always meet certain fans' astronomically high expectations.

Nostalgia is an important but mixed emotion, says Tim Wulf, at the Centre for Advanced Internet Studies in Bochum, Germany. It has positive aspects but also a kind of sadness, too. Happiness in remembering things that were important to us, and also an awareness that that time is now gone.

"It's a social emotion because memories that induce nostalgia for us are connected to other people," he explains, adding that nostalgia can boost our self-esteem while highlighting key social bonds.

Video games, says Wulf, are notoriously good at triggering nostalgia because gamers can "jump into the same environment". In other words, games are like time machines for our emotions, and returning to fondly remembered places can feel stabilising or reassuring in an ever-changing world.

Doran, for one, notes that when you return to a favourite old game, there's something satisfying about deploying previously learned skills. You know what to do here, you're winning again. Just like the old days.

Video games are good at triggering nostalgic feelings says Tim Wulf

Putting out remasters and remakes has been a "really successful" strategy for the games industry, says Piers Harding-Rolls, head of games research at Ampere Analysis.

"Tying in with established audiences and building on existing content is quite a safe route for a lot of companies," he adds. In many cases, it is cheaper to update an existing game rather than create a new title from scratch.

Among other remakes that have found big audiences recently are those of Resident Evil 4 and Assassin's Creed Black Flag.

But Ocarina of Time perhaps stands out because, ever since its original release, it has been considered by many as one of the greatest games ever made. "The interest from all the Gen Zs that I work with is really high," says Harding-Rolls. The legend of The Legend of Zelda, as it were.

Since 2007 there have been 14 main releases of Assassin's Creed

Clearly, nostalgia is not the only driver of interest in the remake, and today's audiences will expect a game that plays to today's standards.

Games companies must keep innovating when linking contemporary audiences to the past, says Wulf, in order satisfy their desire for fresh experiences. And although Wulf himself was a big fan of the original, he has no personal interest in the Ocarina of Time remake. "I want a new game â I don't want to play an old game," he says. Nostalgia isn't always enough for some.

Plus, critics often highlight a tension between originals and remakes. Which is the definitive version? In 2021, Square Enix released the bizarrely titled Nier Replicant ver.1.22474487139... â a remaster of a game first published in 2010. One reviewer wrote, external, "What we're left with is two nearly identical Niers each vying to be the true Nier."

Fans may react badly should a remaster or remake fail to do justice to the source material. And, in the gaming world, angry fan reactions can be serious.

"There's always going to be that fan base or group of people that are going to nit-pick," says Stephanie Ijoma, chief executive and founder of NNESAGA, a consultancy and creative agency.

Ultimately, games developers working on remakes have to "find the right balance" between appealing to fans and allowing themselves to be creative with the material, she adds. "You can't make art in fear, you can't create in fear."

Ijoma gives the example of Final Fantasy VII. First released in 1997, it got a highly anticipated remake in 2020.

This was a game Ijoma played back in the late 1990s and she was thrilled to be able to share it with younger players today.

First released in 1997, Final Fantasy VII got a remake in 2020

Remakes enable this sharing of experiences because getting old computer hardware or video games consoles to work, while a pastime loved by some, is not possible for everyone.

"I've tried multiple times to play Silent Hill 2 because I'm interested in older games but it's such a difficult game to come into nowadays," says Niklas Hallin, a solo indie game developer who runs the studio Neckbolt. "They made a remake and it's so smooth, it just works. It works on your modern hardware."

Hallin did not play many games as a child so he says nostalgia isn't much of a motivator for him. However, in his own games, he likes to include subtle stylistic references to classic games because he knows that this appeals to gamers.

Indie developers often lean on retro games aesthetics not just because people respond well to them â but also because they are easier to achieve. "You can't compete with billion dollar budgets, super high-fidelity, Triple A graphics," he says.

One question remains. The visual difference, today, between games released years apart is, arguably, negligible. So will there be any point in remaking a game from 2026 in, say, 2038? Will technology have moved on so much by then that it's actually worth it?

Harding-Rolls predicts that games companies will still seek to connect new audiences with classic titles by offering updates.

Remakes might become less about graphical improvements and more about editorial adjustments. "Reinterpretations," he says. "Or versions with additional content."

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

#220. Lucid misses Wall Street’s earnings expectations, expands loan from Saudi Arabia fund

Lucid Group missed Wall Street's third-quarter expectations as the embattled electric vehicle maker continues an "operational reset" aimed at cutting costs and narrowing losses.

The company on Monday also announced a $1.75 billion increase in a delayed draw term loan from Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the company's largest shareholder.

Lucid stock fell roughly 5% in aftermarket trading Monday.

Here's how Lucid performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Despite the top- and bottom-line misses, the company's adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of a loss of $726 million beat Wall Street's expectations of a loss of $611 million for the third quarter. Its net loss for the quarter was $830.9 million, down from a loss of $978.4 million a year earlier.

Lucid's 2025 third quarter included revenue of $336.6 million, a loss of $3.31 per share and an adjusted EBITDA loss of $717.7 million.

The quarterly results are the first since Lucid CEO Silvio Napoli, who started leading the company in June, announced a restructuring plan that included implementing several cost-cutting efforts and delaying the company's upcoming midsize vehicle from the end of this year until the second half of next.

"Our transformation is beginning to show progress. We are acting with urgency, strengthening the fundamentals and driving greater discipline, while continuing to invest in the priorities to deliver profitable long-term growth," Napoli said in a release.

The company's turnaround plan includes $1.4 billion in cash flow improvements this year as well as focusing attention on a robotaxi program with Uber and Nuro, a factory that's under construction in Saudi Arabia and the midsize vehicle.

Lucid said the capital from PIF is expected to give the company "additional financial flexibility as Lucid delivers on its turnaround program." It is expected to assist in funding "operations and key investments" into the fourth quarter of 2027, including the start of production for the midsize vehicle.

The additional funds boost Lucid's total liquidity to $3.75 billion, up from $2 billion to end the third quarter.

"We are grateful for the continued financial support from our anchor shareholder, which further energizes us to remain focused on execution and on our commitments as we build a stronger, sustainable Lucid," Napoli said.

Lucid said Monday that the company expects to communicate its midterm plan and targets early next year. The EV maker in May suspended its guidance and many targets, citing new leadership and time needed to develop a turnaround plan.

#219. Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

Managing AI agents is a big part of Lotte Underwood's job

Recent university graduate Lotte Underwood is a public relations executive with a twist - she spends more time using AI than actually doing PR.

Underwood, 25, joined London-based PR agency Fight or Flight in November last year, in the new role of "AI associate" after completing a degree and then a masters in marketing.

She's part of a recently established, dedicated AI team that has built around 30 AI agents. Specific tasks include industry research, client reporting, media interview preparation, helping clients create clearer briefs, and image generation.

A big part of Underwood's job is to manage those agents, check their outputs and feed those to the team members who are dealing directly with clients and the media.

Another key responsibility of hers is to monitor how clients appear in the responses from AI tools like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and others.

"A huge part is being in client meetings and calls to work out what is actually going to be most helpful from these agents," says Underwood.

AI is seen as one of the main factors behind graduate job cuts, but companies are also creating dedicated AI-specific graduate roles like Underwood's.

Dillan Patel, for example, joined engineering software company Stride 18 months ago as an intern. A business management graduate, he was put to work developing an internal chatbot designed to capture the knowledge of a senior staff member transitioning into retirement.

Patel, 24, is now an AI automation analyst, tasked with developing further internal agents to provide specific information to individual teams, while also identifying which manual or repetitive tasks across the company could be done by AI.

"My role focuses on finding practical ways to use technology, automation, data and AI to improve how we operate," says Coventry-based Patel.

Debra Maxwell is looking for "AI disruptors"

Such AI savvy graduates are also in demand from companies like ArvatoConnect, an outsourced contact centre company.

CEO Debra Maxwell says the company's usual graduate intake is on hold this year, but she plans to resume it next year with a bigger focus on AI.

"We would be looking for more AI-disruptor type roles, where you can challenge us as an organisation to think more holistically about where AI can be applied and where we could grow our business from," says London-based Maxwell.

"We see our graduates as not filling administrative roles, but actually adding value to the organisation in a different way."

Maxwell adds: "For example, one of our recent graduates is using AI to do deep dives on specific markets, and provide more information for when we're bidding [for new contracts]. It's basically giving him a more rounded role."

If AI can make a graduate job bigger and more integral to the organisation, Maxwell argues that companies can create a more loyal, engaged workforce that is able to contribute more meaningfully, much earlier.

Research from consultancy PwC found that some graduate roles are assuming more responsibilities because of AI, as it allows companies to demand more senior abilities from junior hires.

For the consultancy's 2026 AI Jobs Barometer, external, it analysed 2.4 million entry-level jobs in the US. It found that the entry-level roles most influenced by AI are now seven times more likely to require traditionally senior-level skills. These include motivational leadership, strategic decision making and team building.

Those entry-level roles with higher expectations have in fact grown 35% since 2019, PwC found, while other entry-level roles actually shrank by 10%.

Cian Clinton uses around ten AI agents for work tasks

Cian Clinton, who joined consultancy Valliance last September after graduating from a Bachelor of Science in 2024, says the incorporation of AI into his role makes him feel less like a graduate, and more like an equal team member. He uses around 10 AI agents daily for different tasks.

"Your ideas and your creativity are a lot more valued because you actually get to implement those firsthand, and the responsibility you take on is a lot greater because of that," says London-based Clinton, 23.

"I am now taking on responsibilities which would usually take me four or five years to get to."

His career progression, he believes, will now be faster as a result.

"AI is killing the 'pyramid' approachâ¦progression is no longer based on your knowledge bank. Now it's more based on your critical thinking and understanding of the processes," says Clinton.

There's no doubt Underwood, Patel and Clinton are developing valuable AI knowledge which can help them across their careers.

But with their time at work so focused on AI, does it matter how much - or how little - time they're spending learning about the professional field they're supposed to be operating in?

Underwood says she'd like to still pick up the traditional PR skills her role isn't currently focused on, but is confident of her future in the agency's growing AI team.

"There's certain things that I haven't had the chance to do yet, like directly pitching to the media. I absolutely plan to do this," she says.

Graduates becoming too AI-centric is an issue for Simon Pratt, chief operating officer at Portman Finance Group. He says being able to use AI effectively is important, but the company's two-year graduate programme is fully centred on "learning the trade".

"Our graduates rotate across four areas at six-month intervals, working directly alongside senior relationship managers to learn their trade," Northampton-based Pratt explains.

"They'll learn all of our financial products, how to draft credit papers for submission to lenders, and how to build meaningful relationships with our customers. They'll progress to running transactions from start to finish."

Claire Reid, PwC UK's chief technology and innovation officer, believes graduate roles that are too AI heavy won't necessarily provide graduates long-term career progression.

"There will be no graduate job now that doesn't require a baseline of AI literacy," says Glasgow-based Reid.

"But AI skills on their own are not going to help you in your career. You've got to couple that with that sector knowledge of what that client actually wants."

Back in Coventry, Dillan Patel is confident his AI work will continue to be valuable, and he wants to keep building on it.

"I don't think I'd want to explore different positions within the business. Through my involvement in AI-related projects, I already work closely with different areas of the business and I'm gaining valuable knowledge and experience along the way," he says.

#218. Why Singapore keeps turning up in the fight over Nvidia chips and China

Singapore, a hub for billions of dollars' worth of Nvidia sales each year, finds itself increasingly in the crosshairs of Washington's push to stop advanced AI chips from reaching China, as the chipmaker CEO's scandal once again thrusts the city-state into the spotlight.

The Southeast Asian nation said Thursday that it had barred Alice Huang, the businesswoman at the center of a scandal that catalyzed the end of Jensen Huang's run as Nvidia CEO, from re-entering the country.

A fifth person was handed 41 charges on Friday in a Singapore fraud case involving computer servers that might have contained Nvidia chips and were exported to Malaysia. The alleged transactions in the broader case totaled about $390 million, prosecutors said March last year.

The investigations into several companies and key officials, including Megaspeed International, began in February 2025 and remained ongoing as of Thursday.

Authorities have been examining whether servers exported through Singapore to Malaysia without entering the domestic market were subsequently rerouted to another destination, contrary to declarations made in Singapore, the Ministry of Home Affairs said in a statement late Thursday.

Singapore has faced growing international scrutiny, particularly from the U.S., over its role as a major billing and potential transit hub for Nvidia chips that U.S. officials say have reached China.

The U.S. has imposed export restrictions on Nvidia's most cutting-edge AI chips to China since 2022 to curb Beijing's surging AI development.

Nvidia's revenue linked to Singapore rose to 22% in the fiscal quarter ending July 2025, up from 8% in 2023, with the country's trade ministry estimating chips physically delivered there accounted for less than 1% of the chipmaker's revenue.

Experts have pointed to gaps in enforcement of U.S. export restrictions and Singapore's corporate system that make it hard to trace where advanced Nvidia chips ultimately end up.

Nvidia chips can be legally sold to customers in other countries, such as Malaysia or Singapore, and then illicitly resold or re-exported to China. Chinese companies can also legally gain remote access to the most advanced AI chips hosted in data centers outside China rather than buying the hardware directly.

Chris McGuire, a senior fellow at Council on Foreign Relations and former U.S. national security official, said that Southeast Asia has become "a major hub for chip smuggling and export control circumvention," importing tens of billions of dollars' worth of AI chips per year.

Singapore has stepped up efforts to police suspected chip diversion since last year, including investigations involving alleged fraud and money laundering.

Authorities acknowledged the potential "blind spots between separate monitoring of trade flows and banking flows," said Alvin Camba, lead scientist and director of research at Lyvi, an Austin-based AI risk assessment firm. Correlating physical shipments with related financial transactions can be "challenging," he added.

"Singapore's strong corporate secrecy regulations, which offer extensive protections for foreign investors, can also make it difficult to rapidly identify the true recipients of goods," Camba said.

Those challenges can be exploited to "obfuscate financial flows and potentially circumvent global restrictions on technology transfers," he added, urging constant adaptation from regulators.

Singapore said it had received limited help from U.S. authorities in its yearlong investigation.

Police sought information on Super Micro Computer Inc., a U.S. server supplier relevant to the probe, but the company provided limited cooperation, according to the Thursday statement.

The city-state first raised the issue with U.S. authorities in July 2025, and has repeated its request on several occasions since September last year. "Despite these repeated requests, Singapore has to date received limited information or assistance in respect of the company," it said.

Police interviewed Alice Huang, a Hong Kong resident who was then Megaspeed's CEO, chairperson and a key controller, in June 2025.

In February 2026, authorities concluded that there was insufficient evidence that she had committed offenses related to the declarations under investigation. She was nonetheless barred from re-entering Singapore after being found providing inaccurate information to downplay her role in Megaspeed.

The country does not enforce "unilateral export controls" imposed by other countries, the ministry said in Thursday's statement, but it expects companies operating there to take foreign export rules into account in their international business activities.

Megaspeed, which was spun off from a Chinese gaming company, became Nvidia's largest Southeast Asian customer in less than three years and has bought more than $4.8 billion worth of its chips since June 2024, according to The New York Times, though the company has denied any wrongdoing.

Alice Huang, whom U.S. intelligence identified as a Chinese intelligence asset passing information to Beijing, served as managing director at Megaspeed for eight months in 2023 but has reportedly continued to play a leading role at the company.

Jensen Huang has resigned as Nvidia CEO on Thursday, ending 33 years at the helm after revelations that he had two extramarital affairs, plunging the chipmaker at the heart of the global AI boom into crisis.

Nvidia appointed Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress as interim CEO while its board searches for a permanent successor.

#214. The engine parts that 'fight the laws of the universe'

A jet engine will have hundreds of turbine blades

Every time you fly, an extraordinary feat of endurance occurs inside the aircraft's engines.

Hidden from view inside the sleek outer nacelle are hundreds of little turbine blades that can shrug off enormous mechanical stresses and temperatures exceeding 1,500C.

It is a true engineering marvel. And these hardy blades are not sculpted or cut from their material â they are grown.

During the 1960s, engineers worked out how to take a molten metal alloy, cast it into the shape of a turbine blade, and then cool it very carefully from one end to the other over a matter of hours. A single crystal or grain of metal alloy forms during this process of gradual, delicate solidification.

Like turning a cup of water into one big snowflake, of your own design, rather than a mini blizzard of many smaller snowflakes.

"It's about as complicated as manufacturing gets," says Ben Fifoot, manufacturing engineer at Rolls-Royce, which makes turbine blades in the UK. "It's a real motivator to get out of bed in the morning."

Only a handful of companies around the world can produce these single crystal turbine blades, and the process has been perfected end-to-end in just five countries, including the US and UK. Engine-makers continually vie for access to the technology.

Rolls-Royce, GE and France's Safran are among the few firms that have perfected the process.

In September, GE Aerospace announced, external it would acquire its turbine blade supplier for $11.75bn (Â£8.78bn). Neither company responded to BBC News' requests for comment but the move has been described as an effort to ensure supply of these critical components.

Separately, in March, Rolls-Royce said it would invest more than Â£19m in its Rotherham blade casting facility.

Turbine blade specialists continue to work on improving the capabilities of this little-known technology. All the while, China is desperate to prove that its engineers can produce turbine blades of the same quality as those in the West. Who will come out on top?

"Demand for [turbine blades] is huge. So few companies have managed to do it, which does give it this kind of magical aura," says Bill Sweetman, an aviation industry writer and consultant.

He suspects GE Aerospace's recent move is about securing reliability and predictability in the notoriously volatile aircraft manufacturing supply chain.

The casts play a crucial part in the cooling process

But why is the single crystal casting process, which has been around for decades now, still so specialist?

To grow one of these blades, asserts Fifoot, is to "fight the laws of the universe".

This is because each molten blade would naturally cool into a solid object consisting of many small grains of metal alloy, all jumbled together. But you don't want a crumbly sugar cube, you want a perfect diamond.

Getting a single crystal to grow into the shape of the blade instead isn't just a matter of cooling the molten version down slowly, the process also uses a bizarre trick called a pigtail.

The pigtail is a small spiral at one end of the blade. Fifoot says that, at the beginning of the cooling process, this spiral "chokes" the flow of solidifying crystals so that just one takes over. A bit like a hairpin corner in an F1 race right before the finish line â one driver can take the lead here and go on to win.

"Sometimes we need to play around with the number of turns or the angles on [the pigtail] to try and get more favourable grain," adds Fifoot. Once the blade is formed, this crucial but sacrificial spiral is cut off.

France's Safran is among the few companies that can make single crystal blades

Blades made by Rolls-Royce are used in aircraft engines including the Trent XWB-84, which powers the Airbus A350-900, and the Trent 1000 XE, for the Boeing 787.

The single crystal is essential to ensure each blade's performance. Sometimes a blade doesn't cool quite as planned, for example two crystals form within it instead of just one, and the metal alloy must be reprocessed so that the manufacturer may re-use the material.

Special ceramic coatings also protect blades from extreme temperatures inside engines. And another important feature is that blades are cast with tiny channels that allow a flow of cooler air to coat them in a kind of film while the aircraft engine is working.

"The drive at the moment [across the industry] is currently to make those cooling passages really intricate," says Paul Withey, at the University of Birmingham, who formerly worked for Rolls-Royce. The more turbulent those little flows of cool air are, he explains, the more they will knock heat out of the surrounding, much hotter air.

And blade-makers also seek to reduce the mass of these components as much as possible.

"If we can take any weight out of it, you can run that engine more efficiently using less fuel â you can spin it faster and hotter," says Fifoot.

The turbine blades are used in the Trent 1000 engine among others

Meanwhile, China hopes to become only the sixth country to master the whole technology chain for single crystal blade casting.

A report in May, external quoted one engineer at the AECC Beijing Institute of Aeronautical Materials claiming that China now had a single crystal alloy "superior to or equivalent to the second-generation single-crystal superalloys widely used in Europe and the US". It costs less to make, too, apparently.

But the West has a "huge advantage" over China given that the majority of commercial airliner production happens there, says Sweetman. Supply chains and the latest innovations are generally clustered in the West. Plus, Western manufacturers also have lengthy safety records under their belts.

China must compete on practically every aspect of aircraft manufacturing, external â by no means just on turbine blades â in order to make serious in-roads. "You can definitely see that happening but it's a long, uphill struggle," adds Sweetman.

Demand for aircraft is currently high, which is already straining various supply chains in the industry. That demand could grow higher still, suggests Withey.

During the next few years, one of the two main aircraft manufacturers, Airbus and Boeing, could launch a new short-haul plane, external, he says. Then, the other would have to follow suit in order to remain competitive.

"There'll be a bun fight," says Withey, over who gets to supply the engines and, by extension, those all-important single crystal turbine blades.

#211. Analysis: Nvidia’s leadership crisis tests the chipmaker’s role as the ‘central bank of AI’

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's shock resignation is raising a worrying question: Could trouble at the chipmaker spread through the tightly connected financial network Nvidia has built to support the artificial intelligence boom?

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

Serious problems with Nvidia could do far-reaching economic and financial damage. The company has made hundreds of billions of dollars in financial commitments to other firms to help the industry grow, earning it the nickname the "central bank of AI." The Federal Reserve and other financial institutions are closely watching the AI buildout to monitor for the possibility that an unexpected event could destabilize the global financial system.

Even back in July Fed officials were discussing the possibility of a "broad-based repricing of assets" in the event of problems in the AI sector.

A rerun of the 2007-2008 financial crisis isn't likely, according to analysts and current and former financial officials. Nvidia isn't "too big to fail in a bank sense at all," said Sheila Bair, a former government regulator who ran the Federal Deposit Insurance Corp. Unlike a troubled bank, Nvidia isn't vulnerable to a sudden loss of confidence and would still produce a product customers want to buy even if something went wrong. And its massive equity gives it a cushion.

But regulators need to watch carefully for exposure by the banks and the wider financial system to Nvidia and other firms like it, Bair said.

"Nvidia going down would be a pretty seismic event," she said.

Investors still have high hopes for Nvidia, which reports earnings on Tuesday. Analysts expect its revenue to more than double in the current fiscal year, and then to roughly double again over the following two years, according to LSEG data. But Huang's exit from the company Thursday following a pair of extramarital affairs, including one with a woman The New York Times reported was suspected of spying for China, makes the company's fate extraordinarily hard to predict. Huang had led the company since co-founding it in 1993.

Nvidia didn't respond to a request for comment. The company named CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO.

Nvidia's stock fell 33% from its Oct. 6 peak through Thursday morning. Still, people who closely track the financial flows underlying AI say that even sharper losses by Nvidia likely wouldn't trigger the kind of systemic problems that regulators guard against.

And yet the risks flowing from AI are keeping some finance officials up at night. They are watching for the possibility that many of the factors that have helped the AI boom could reverse simultaneously. If the value of Nvidia's GPU chips fell, for instance, it could prompt investors to ask whether companies that borrowed money to buy them would be able to pay off their debts.

In that case, the financial assistance big tech firms have extended to others in the industry could suddenly become drags on their growth. Tech companies would have credit problems at the same time that their equity is falling. Problems among giant tech firms wouldn't stay contained to tech investors' portfolios, but could cause problems across the economy.

"I think that's a real concern because both the economy and financial markets are standing on the same leg, which means a drawdown could have reinforcing effects between the two," said Jason Wu, an assistant director at the International Monetary Fund who oversees the Global Markets Analysis unit.

Wu was the lead author of a chapter of the IMF's Global Financial Stability report published in October that warned about the potential for growing leverage among AI firms and investors to trigger broader problems. The so-called hyperscalers — the biggest tech firms — have grown from just 1% of bond issuance in the $15 trillion investment-grade debt market to 10% in just two years. Hedge funds and retail investors are making risky bets even riskier by taking on debt to buy stocks or investing in funds that have embedded leverage.

Nvidia is at the center of all of that. The company has repeatedly stepped in to help backstop smaller AI companies known as neoclouds, which build data centers using Nvidia chips and lease capacity to other firms. Among other obligations, Nvidia disclosed in July that it has committed to buy up to $36 billion in cloud services from those companies. That helps Nvidia ensure that there will be more customers for its AI chips, and it helps those companies find easier financing.

Wu and others refer to these kinds of arrangements as circular financing. When business is growing, it can be productive for bigger firms to help their customers buy their products. But if Nvidia couldn't honor its obligations, it would ripple through the firms that borrowed the money and the banks that lent to it. Relatively opaque private credit funds have also lent heavily into the AI boom, and how they would fare is a subject of debate among regulators.

Nvidia didn't reveal its specific partners, but some neoclouds such as CoreWeave and IREN have separately disclosed their relationships with the bigger firm.

Many neoclouds have been under stress in recent weeks. The cost of insuring CoreWeave debt, as measured by credit-default spreads, rose by 94% — nearly doubling — from Oct. 1 to Nov. 5, before settling, according to LSEG data. The price of an IREN convertible bond maturing in 2029 fell 23% from Nov. 3 to Nov. 5, amid a 26% decline in the company's share price.

Nvidia's other obligations include a $105 billion phased guarantee to a major data center project in Ohio planned by OpenAI and SB Energy, subject to certain conditions. Nvidia promised to backstop up to $125 billion of a $500 billion credit program by Wall Street financial firms.

These obligations come in different forms and are unlikely to come due all at once, if ever. Stijn Van Nieuwerburgh, a Columbia Business School finance professor, estimates them at $400 billion overall.

That is a huge sum, but still a small fraction of the roughly $3.9 trillion market capitalization Nvidia has even after its stock decline. "The company would have to get to the brink of extinction before all these guarantees come into jeopardy."

"I don't want to say it's a safe system, but I do want to say there's a lot of equity in the system right now," Van Nieuwerburgh said.

But in the past few days, Nvidia's troubles have separated it from the pack. Most neoclouds' equity prices have recovered this week as Nvidia's share price has fallen. That suggests investors see Nvidia's problems as specific to the company, for now.

Meanwhile the basic laws of economics are driving other firms to try to move into Nvidia's lucrative chips business. AMD, Broadcom, Intel, Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI all have efforts underway to build their own AI chips, Van Nieuwerburgh said.

Over time, they could replace Nvidia's products. That could cap the economic fallout of any potential troubles at the company.

And yet it's impossible to entirely write off the financial risks of the AI trade suddenly hitting a wall, added Van Nieuwerburgh, who spoke to me over Zoom from the sidelines of a bimonthly meeting of finance officials in Basel, Switzerland.

Central bankers "are clearly very, very, very anxious about this and thinking hard about these questions," he said.

#209. Robot vessels take on the dangerous work of mine hunting

This South Korean vessel is among the ships damaged in the Strait of Hormuz

Traversing the Strait of Hormuz remains one of the most dangerous trips a vessel can take.

Despite assistance from the US Navy, ships are still being attacked.

"To transit the Strait of Hormuz you need agreement between the captain and the crew to take the risk. You need war risk insurance. And the captain has to make the final decision," says Steve Cameron, a former merchant navy officer and maritime consultant.

Missiles and drones are a threat from the air, but under the surface mines may also be lurking.

"If you're a captain, you'd like to see evidence mines aren't present," Cameron adds.

Modern mines rest on the seabed and detonate when they detect an acoustic or magnetic signal, triggering a pressure wave that will break the keel of a passing vessel.

Others can rise through shallow waters or float, or be powered towards a target.

The technology itself is not difficult or costly, but the mere threat can stop a tanker fleet from sailing.

Ian McFarlane of French defence group Thales is a naval architect who knows mine warfare well. "Mines are a very cheap way of waging a naval war, as the Royal Navy is acutely aware," he says.

Finding sea mines has traditionally been the job of specialist mine-hunting vessels, with crews of around 80 sailors.

To keep humans out of harm's way, an increasing amount of the work is done by systems that are autonomous and monitored from the shore.

They might only need 20 personnel, working in shifts on land. With the recruitment and retention challenges facing Nato navies these highly-portable robot systems are very attractive.

Such tech has been under development for 20 years.

During the 2003 Iraq war the US Navy poked around the seabed looking for mines with a torpedo-shaped robot called the Remus 100.

Pinging around with sonar these little machines operated down to around 100ft depth and spared divers a difficult and dangerous task.

Iran's military published this picture of a US submersible captured in the Gulf

The latest conflict in the Gulf is testing much more capable systems.

The US military is sparing with details about their mine-hunting craft, but we have seen glimpses of them.

Iranian forces hauled an uncrewed US autonomous sub, external out of the waters of the Strait of Hormuz in early September.

The Pentagon claimed this robot, an Extra Large Uncrewed Underwater Vehicle (XLUUV) from US autonomous defence tech venture Anduril, was a malfunctioning older device with no classified equipment on-board.

Whatever its mission, no sailors were imperilled. That, says William Lupton, a naval analyst with Shephard Group, "was a drop in the ocean compared to having prisoners taken", and part of the point of uncrewed vessels.

What if an uncrewed vessel is captured? Nato navies are reluctant to go into details, but computer drives can be wiped or destroyed remotely by various means. This may have taken place inside the humming circuit boards of the XLUUV seized by Iran.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle is packed with sensors

Such autonomous tech is being embraced by forces around the world.

Cooperation between Thales, the Royal Navy, and France's Marine Nationale has produced a robot motor launch.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle (USV) is packed with sensors programmed to detect mines via sonar that was developed for the oil and gas industries.

It tows a 5m long rectangular box, similar to a large torpedo, that takes a hi-resolution look at the seabed and creates a 3D picture refined by AI. The latest mines are designed to blend into their surroundings and the software spots anything that looks like a mine.

Autonomous systems are cheaper than conventional minehunters, plus they can be deployed, along with containerised control centres, by air.

Steve Prest, associate fellow at the RUSI defence think tank, introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy and was its first fleet robotics officer, a title he acquired in 2014.

"There is a bunch of ways to look for a mine using robot boats or robot subs, and while you can't prove the negative, that no mines are there, you can reduce the risk."

He says US robot minehunters can either blow up a mine or just mark the spot as somewhere to avoid.

"We'll see more use of autonomy but also the missions and capability will expand. And AI will be analysing data from those vessels on the spot as opposed to data being analysed when a drone returns."

The Royal Navy recently announced its concept of a hybrid navy - mixing drones with conventional warships. But Prest thinks for this vision to succeed the fleet must operate as one distributed force, sharing data in what he terms a cognitive architecture.

The robot only makes a real difference as part of this scheme and it's a mistake to focus on individual uncrewed assets. "Uncrewed or autonomous vessels are not the operating system. They are applications running on it."

Steve Prest introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy

While robots are on the rise, people are what really matter in the Strait.

Neil Roberts speaks for insurers at Lloyd's of London on marine matters and says the psychological impact of the mine threat is inescapable, as is the uncertainty around just how many mines, if any, are out there.

"The US says it has cleared the Omani route and believes the mine threat is being managed, with shipments running at about 75% of pre-conflict levels."

Fear of the unknown is the most potent aspect of the sea mine threat and robots are now the weapon of choice to confront that fear.

#207. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

#206. Nscale updates IPO prospectus that shows widening loss

Nvidia-backed neocloud Nscale on Thursday released updated paperwork for a U.S. initial public offering.

The company had a third-quarter net loss of $1.69 billion on $307.5 million in revenue, which was up 1,344% year over year, according to an updated prospectus for its planned initial public offering. The net loss widened from $1.02 billion in the second quarter, when the company generated $140.6 million in revenue, up 1,252%.

London-based Nscale filed to go public in September, but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company did not specify how much it intends to raise in the public offering on the New York Stock Exchange.

Since then the company has faced public setbacks.

Microsoft walked away from an Nscale data center that it had planned to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

The neocloud market, which focuses on renting access to chips such as Nvidia graphics processing units to clients, has been in tumult, partly because Jensen Huang resigned as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, after admitting to being unfaithful to his wife. Nvidia is investigating Huang's relationship with a woman whom intelligence agencies have called a Chinese spy.

In October investors pressured shares of Nscale rival CoreWeave, but the stock advanced almost 25% on Wednesday on strong results. CoreWeave pulled back about 7% on Thursday following the departure of Huang, who has overseen investments in the neocloud.

Shares of another neocloud, Nebius, fell about 17% on Thursday, with the company issuing light full-year guidance.

But Nscale can point to growing business with Anthropic, which itself is aiming to go public. In late September Anthropic exercised its right to contract for additional computing power at an Nscale site in West Virginia.

#204. The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

BBC's Samira Hussain reports from Wall Street on what the fall of the tech superstar means.

Jensen Huang, known for sporting a black leather jacket to shareholder events, appeared to have it all. He was courted by leaders in the US and China alike, worshipped by investors and seen as a loyal family man. But a rockstar approach to his personal life has ultimately led to his resignation.

The 63-year-old has been married since 1985, long before the foundation of Nvidia, the company that propelled him to tech stardom.

He is known as a workaholic and a demanding manager, with high standards for those who worked for him, which included his own children, promoted within the company. But he has seen his personal reputation go into freefall this week, undone by romantic entanglements that throw doubt on his focus and his judgement.

On Thursday, he resigned following back-to-back revelations of two affairs. News of the first - a relationship with a businesswoman alleged to be a Chinese spy - ricocheted around the world, raising eyebrows among investors. It was followed on Wednesday by reports that the tech boss had also had an affair with an Nvidia colleague.

His departure creates uncertainty in the global stock market, which was already knocked-off balance in recent weeks. Nvidia is a linchpin in the US's race for technology leadership with China.

Huang may not be a household name on the same level as Elon Musk or Mark Zuckerberg.

But his role in the tech industry, especially in relation to the explosion of artificial intelligence in the last three years, may prove more consequential. Nvidia dominates the market for the microchips that are used to power the new technology.

The company, which is still valued at nearly $4n (Â£3.02tn) despite recent events, is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

Huang enjoyed access to the seats of political power on an unrivalled scale, being courted by both US President Donald Trump and China's President Xi Jinping.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession has led to him being dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" and seen him amass an estimated fortune of $145bn, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

Despite always donning his trademark all-black outfits and taking a playful approach to investor presentations, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a Master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work in senior roles at Nvidia.

The company became a key provider of the computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in a new direction. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing AI technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on par with the advent of electricity. Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company and its customers include the biggest firms on the planet, Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic, all spending big on its products.

So it is no surprise that at a recent AI summit at the White House, Huang was picked out from a group of 42 to flank the president, alongside Elon Musk, both men with whom Trump has famously close relationships.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Jensen Huang, told the BBC, referring to the late boss of Apple. He added that Huang was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

He described him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".

But while the former Nvidia CEO is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well", he said.

Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, he added, something that was "very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," Witt added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

First reports emerged on 29 October in the New York Times that Huang had conducted a relationship with Alice Huang, no relation. US officials allege the businesswoman associated with Singapore-based firm Megaspeed is a Chinese intelligence asset.

After the revelations of the first affair, Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

Trump, as well as other leaders in the AI tech industry, initially backed Huang to continue in his role at Nvidia. The president described him as "honourable".

But the reporting of a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare who has worked at Nvidia for 18 years, prompted a board meeting, followed by the announcement Huang was stepping down.

In the aftermath of that news Trump said Huang had done "a fantastic job" and proposed Tim Cook of Apple as a suitable replacement at the head of Nvidia.

#201. Australia considers criminal penalties for rogue AI in wake of OpenAI hack

PM Anthony Albanese says the law needs to catch up with a "fast-changing world"

Australia plans to create a new AI safety regulator and has flagged criminal penalties for tech companies who do not comply, months after an OpenAI hack.

In September, Australia's Prime Minister Anthony Albanese revealed that a rogue OpenAI agent had "infiltrated" a government health website and other systems, and criticised the company for taking months to tell them about the hack.

On Tuesday, he released the 18 recommendations made after a "rapid review" into the breaches including new safety and security standards for AI companies such as mandatory reporting of hacks.

AI brings opportunities, he said, but Australia must prepare for the "new risks" from this "rapidly advancing technology".

"The law needs to get ahead of and, in some cases, make sure that it catches up with, what is a very fast changing world," Albanese said.

One of the recommendations suggests changes to criminal law to have a standalone "failure to prevent" offence .

This would criminalise instances where a company develops "an AI model that engages in harmful conduct" and "fails to take reasonable steps to prevent that harm".

The review also recommended "mandatory, time-bound AI safety reporting" of hacks.

This point addresses what Albanese had earlier called his "disappointment" in the months-long delay for OpenAI to tell his government about the breach.

In June, a statistics portal containing "non-sensitive" data from Australia's universal healthcare scheme Medicare was hacked by a rogue OpenAI agent.

OpenAI said it only learnt of the breach in August while reviewing "misaligned model activity" and emailed a general inbox of an Australian government agency on 10 September.

It took several more days before the email was escalated to Australia's cybersecurity centre, with a message then sent to a government minister ahead of the prime minister being told.

Albanese said he spoke to OpenAI boss Sam Altman and raised "Australia's extreme concern about this incident", with Altman acknowledging there were "issues with protocols" at OpenAI.

Days after the hacks were revealed, OpenAI delayed its rollout of a new AI model, citing safety concerns.

Why did an OpenAI system hack Australia's health system - and can it be stopped in the future?

OpenAI scraps rollout of new model over safety concerns
