#198. Nvidia: Ex-prosecutor Joon Kim and Wachtell, Lipton bring big track record to investigation

Joon Kim, the lawyer tapped by artificial intelligence chip giant Nvidia to investigate former CEO Jensen Huang, and the Big Law firm he works for have a long history of investigating high-profile cases.

Kim, in his career as a top federal prosecutor and as an attorney in private practice, has had in his sights mobster John "The Teflon Don" Gotti's son, former New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corp. and other high-profile people and companies.

The law firm that Kim recently joined — Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz — this year conducted an investigation for the NBA of the Los Angeles Clippers and its billionaire owner, Steve Ballmer, which ended with the basketball league imposing stiff sanctions on Ballmer and the Clippers for violating salary cap rules in connection with superstar Kawhi Leonard.

On Tuesday, Nvidia said it had retained Kim, 55, and Wachtell, Lipton to "conduct a thorough and independent investigation" of allegations that its then-CEO Jensen Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort involving his affair with a woman, Alice Huang. U.S. intelligence suspects her of being a spy for China.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO on Thursday morning on the heels of a New York Times report that he had also had an extramarital affair with an Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reports to him.

That allegation could be added to the list of issues that Kim will be looking into as part of his probe.

Nvidia did not immediately respond when asked if the parameters of the investigation would be affected by the new claim. A spokeswoman for Wachtell, Lipton declined to comment for this article on behalf of the firm and Kim.

Friends and former colleagues of Kim said Nvidia has made a smart decision in retaining him for the job.

"I think he's the ideal person for this role," said Edward Kim, a New York white-collar defense lawyer who worked in the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Southern District of New York with Joon Kim. The two men are not related.

"I think they're going to get a serious, credible investigation. I believe Joon will follow the facts wherever they will lead," Edward Kim said.

The son of a South Korean diplomat, Joon Kim was born in Los Angeles. He attended Phillips Exeter Academy and later Stanford University.

After graduating cum laude from Harvard Law School, he clerked for a federal judge in Manhattan and then worked as an associate at the major law firm Cleary Gottlieb Steen & Hamilton starting in the late 1990s. After that, he spent seven years in the SDNY as an assistant U.S. attorney. His tenure as a prosecutor there included four years in the Organized Crime and Terrorism Unit.

The SDNY, by virtue of its location in Manhattan, handles some of the most high-profile criminal investigations in the United States, including cases of major financial crimes, government corruption and organized crime.

During his first stint at the SDNY, Kim investigated and prosecuted crimes that included "racketeering, murder, money laundering, securities fraud, firearms and narcotics offenses, tax evasion, and terrorism," the office has previously said.

Kim's trial wins at the SDNY included obtaining a conviction of Peter Gotti, then-head of the Gambino Mafia family, for conspiring to kill notorious mob turncoat Salvatore "Sammy the Bull" Gravano. Kim and his fellow prosecutors were less successful, however, in prosecuting Peter Gotti's nephew, John Gotti Jr., whose trials ended with several hung juries.

"He could be a very tenacious courtroom advocate," said Chris Conniff, a New York attorney who worked with Kim in the SDNY, and who considers Kim one of his closest friends.

"He's incredibly smart," Conniff said. "I think he's known by everyone in the business as just an outstanding investigator, leaves no stone unturned."

Kim in 2006 returned to Cleary Gottlieb as a partner in the firm's litigation and enforcement group but then went back to the SDNY in 2013. During his second stint there, he served as deputy U.S. attorney, and then served as acting U.S. attorney from 2017 to 2018.

Kim's elevation to acting U.S. attorney came as a result of President Donald Trump's firing of then-U.S. Attorney Preet Bharara in March 2017 after Bharara refused the new president's demand that he resign.

"Preet's departure was very unique," and the mood in the SDNY "was shaky" after his ouster, Conniff said.

"Preet was very well-liked and well-respected," said Conniff, who was in private practice at the time that Bharara was fired. "We just lost a very charismatic and effective leader, and in a very dramatic way."

In taking over from Bharara, Kim, "from my external viewpoint, he righted the ship and continued to do the work of the office in the time he was there."

"Joon, I would say, gave people confirmation that it was going to be alright, and it was going to be business as usual," Conniff said.

Kim left the SDNY in April 2018, and began his third stint at Cleary Gottlieb, which he recently left to join Wachtell, Lipton's litigation department.

In March 2021, Kim and another attorney, Anne Clark, a partner at Vladeck, Raskin & Clark, were tapped by New York Attorney General Letitia James to investigate then-Gov. Andrew Cuomo over allegations that he had sexually harassed several women.

"These are serious allegations that demand a rigorous and impartial investigation," Kim said in a statement at the time. "We will act judiciously and follow the facts wherever they lead."

Kim and Clark in August 2021 submitted a 165-page report to James, which concluded that Cuomo had sexually harassed at least 11 women and then retaliated against a former employee who had publicly complained about his behavior. As part of the probe, Kim, Clark and their team interviewed 179 witnesses and reviewed tens of thousands of documents.

Cuomo adamantly denied some of the allegations and said that other claims of alleged misconduct had been mischaracterized or misinterpreted.

A week after the report was publicly released, Cuomo announced that he would resign.

Two years after the Cuomo probe, Kim was retained by the FDIC to conduct an independent review of allegations of sexual harassment and interpersonal misconduct at the agency. As part of the probe, Kim and his team conducted 167 interviews of current and past FDIC employees, and hundreds of additional interviews with individuals who contacted a hotline the team set up for employees to report misconduct claims.

An April 2024 report by Kim and his team found "for far too many employees and for far too long, the FDIC has failed to provide a workplace safe from sexual harassment, discrimination, and other interpersonal misconduct."

More than five years after Cuomo left office, the former governor's spokesman has not forgotten, or forgiven, Kim, whom he accused of writing a report on Cuomo with a predetermined outcome.

Kim was a "hired hack," said the spokesman, Rich Azzopardi.

Azzopardi said Kim "hid evidence" that was exonerating of Cuomo, and accepted at face value "outright lies" from a number of witnesses.

The bad blood between Cuomo's camp and Kim predates the sexual harassment probe.

While working in the SDNY, Kim and Bharara oversaw the investigation that led to the corruption trial of Joseph Percoco, who had been a close aide of Cuomo. The trial took place after both prosecutors had left the office.

Azzopardi cited that prosecution in his scathing remarks about Kim.

Percoco was sentenced to six years in prison after his 2018 conviction for honest-services wire fraud. The conviction was overturned by the U.S. Supreme Court in a unanimous ruling in 2023 — after his release from prison — because the judge's instructions to jurors were too vague.

Azzopardi said that Kim and Bharara were motivated to prosecute Percoco for "their own personal glory" and "to generate headlines for themselves."

Bharara, in a two-word email response about Azzopardi's claim, wrote, "Andrew who?," referring to Cuomo.

Azzopardi said he did not believe there was any other reason for Nvidia's board to retain Kim to investigate the Huang situation other than to reach a predetermined outcome by the board.

"I don't know what the aims of the board are," Azzopardi said.

Kim's friend Conniff, when asked if he expected that the Nvidia investigation would be a whitewash that would serve the board's interests, said, "No."

"I don't think Joon would take an engagement in which he could not provide a fulsome report of what he uncovered during this investigation," Conniff said. "Any company board that is looking to do a good, thorough investigation of potential wrongdoing, Joon's perfect ... for it."

#197. Californians reject wealth tax for billionaires

California voters have voted to reject a wealth tax on its billionaires.

The ballot proposal would have levied a one-time tax on the state's mega wealthy to help fund healthcare. It took nearly a week of counting votes after the midterm election to determine a projection on the measure.

There are about 200 billionaires in the state, including Meta's Mark Zuckerberg, reality television star Kim Kardashian and media mogul Oprah Winfrey.

Proposition 40 targeted Californians with a wealth of more than $1.1bn (Â£800m) with a 5% tax on their total wealth, including assets such as shares as well as income. It failed amid divisions among Democrats and labour unions.

Are California voters about to impose 'world's first' tax on billionaires?

Had it passed, experts believe it would have been the world's first wealth tax targeting billionaires.

The tax would have raised about $100bn to make up for federal funding cuts to healthcare in California as a result of President Donald Trump's sweeping tax and spending bill.

Those who backed the measure had argued it would help ensure that the ultra-wealthy were contributing their fair share and helping backfill gaps in the state's budget.

The wealth tax faced fierce opposition, including from Google co-founder Sergey Brin, as well as California Governor Gavin Newsom and labour unions.

Newsom, whose tenure as governor is coming to an end, had expressed concerns that the measure could cause the wealthy to leave the state and drive out their crucial income tax revenue.

Some billionaire critics of the measure spent millions trying to crush it, with a handful even moving out of the state to evade the possible tax. Brin gave more than $100m to a political organisation funding ballot measures that could block the tax, according to the Los Angeles Times.

Dave Regan, head of the union that led the charge for the measure, said on election night that the billionaires' efforts to block the tax created "a lot of confusion and a lot of second-guessing among people who really in their hearts know what the right the thing to do is".

Regan, who is president of Service Employees International Union-United Healthcare Workers West, pledged to seek a billionaire tax once more in 2028.

The measure had odds stacked against it, with two other propositions on the ballot that would have effectively reversed it, even if it passed.

California, home to some 23 million registered voters, has the largest economy in the country and boasts the fourth largest global economy. It is home to one of the highest concentrations of billionaires in the world, but also has one of the highest wealth gaps.

#193. In a policy switch, the Fed says its October 2027 meeting will not see a vote on interest rates

The Federal Reserve won't vote on a potential interest-rate change at its October 2027 meeting, a departure that is part of a broader initiative from Chairman Kevin Warsh aimed at reducing the central bank's market footprint.

According to an announcement Thursday, the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee will set aside its Oct. 26-27 session for "the discussion of broad monetary policy strategy issues."

Warsh announced the move at his news conference after the FOMC met in October, though he did not specify which meeting would be affected.

In 2027, there will be seven voting meetings, with the October session set up as extra time for policy deliberation without a formal vote or post-meeting statement. The new calendar shows that the final meeting of the year will now take place in November. It had previously been set for December.

"Now, what's the reasoning behind this change? To me, it's quite straightforward," the chairman said in explaining his rationale. "Getting policy right involves assessing near-term developments at timely, regular, and information-rich intervals."

"But it's equally important we reserve time and space, as an intellectually diverse group, for strategic deliberations—for thinking anew about big questions, looking out over longer time horizons, focusing, as I say, on the left side of the decimal point," he said.

Warsh has been critical of the Fed's prior communications strategy, saying policymakers spent too much time providing guidance on policy moves that handcuffed them when it came time to make decisions.

Under typical practice, the Fed meets eight times a year, following with a vote on interest rates and then a news conference from the chair.

Since Warsh took over, the post-meeting statement has been shortened dramatically. Moreover, the press question-and-answer sessions have been cut to around half an hour from their customary 45 minutes or so.

Warsh said in October that the meeting schedule is another step in his plan to streamline communication. He indicated last month that the number of nonvoting meetings would increase to two in 2028.

"I believe the setting of the meeting schedule and agenda is more than some administrative detail," he said. "It's an institutional choice, affecting the nature and the quality of our deliberations and, ultimately, the wisdom of our decisions. It's yet another change to get the conduct of monetary policy on the right track."

The FOMC next meets Dec. 8-9. Its first meeting in 2027 will happen Jan. 26-27.

Correction: The Fed's October 2027 meeting will not see a vote on interest rates. The headline in an earlier version misstated the year.

#192. Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Jensen Huang, 63, has been called the 'Taylor Swift of tech' in the US

The tech world has spent the day digesting the extraordinary news that Jensen Huang - the charismatic, leather-jacket wearing chief executive of the multi-trillion dollar firm Nvidia - is being investigated by his own company over an extramarital affair with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy.

None of us had that on our bingo cards for 2026.

Huang's alleged romantic affair with Alice Huang (no relation), the former CEO of a Singapore-based data centre company, has cast ripples far beyond his immediate family - undoubtedly on an emotional rollercoaster of their own - taking us to a crossroads at which technology, geopolitics and national security intersect.

In a nutshell, this is about the bitter rivalry between the US and China, as well as whether Jensen Huang has betrayed the US as well as his wife.

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

The US is locked in a fierce race with China to develop the best AI models.

"Whoever wins AI, wins," as US President Donald Trump puts it. He thinks that the country with the more dominant AI technology will benefit the most from it and, ultimately, will be the most powerful in the world. He may well be right.

Nvidia happens to make the best chips in the business for AI â so much so that it has been overwhelmed with demand.

It sets its prices accordingly: a single unit of the firm's latest kit for data centres starts at Â£37,000 ($50,000). A medium or large site might have hundreds of them.

The US administration initially slapped an export ban on Nvidia's more recent devices to prevent China from taking advantage of their superior capabilities. It has since softened the rules a little.

But there are suggestions that Alice Huang's company, Megaspeed, may have been involved in sneaking them into the country while she was in charge. Bloomberg reported that the firm bought billions of dollars worth of Nvidia tech in November 2025. Megaspeed denies that it was used illegitimately.

For his part, Jensen Huang has lobbied heavily for the export rules between the two countries to be relaxed, arguing against the weight of general opinion that the AI race doesn't matter and that it would be no bad thing for China to become dependent on US tech.

"President Trump wants American to win and President Xi wants China to win, and it's possible for both of them to do so," he told the BBC last year.

I think it's safe to assume that neither country sees it that way.

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

The difficult questions Nvidia now has to try to answer include whether Alice gained special access to the firm's latest technology, either deliberately or not, via Jensen - and, if so, whether she shared it with Beijing or others.

We don't know when their affair began or ended, but there are numerous photos and videos of Jensen and Alice together at events. It is feasible that there were moments when they spent quite a lot of time together and Jensen has talked about always having an eye on his work.

China denies that she was engaged in espionage.

Interest in economic espionage is neither new nor unique to China. But many Western countries say that China does it on a grand scale, using a combination of technology tools and agents embedded in various organisations - including private companies.

China having access to significant enough numbers of Nvidia's chips to make use of them, or stripping them down and learning how to make its own versions, would infuriate the Trump administration, which is trying to keep its AI technology close to its chest.

Some models have even been withheld from the UK-based AI Safety Institute on the grounds that they are potentially too dangerous to be shared. The US does not want anyone having a headstart or misusing its innovations.

Another potential bombshell lies in the form of any national security information to which Jensen Huang may have been privy.

He is a man operating at the very top of the US elite - one of a handful of people who could probably walk into the White House unannounced and have tea with Trump. He has influence, he has access and he has powerful friends. It's quite possible that secrets were shared with him.

Nvidia says there's no evidence so far that he passed anything on and the firm has launched an internal investigation. Huang says he will co-operate and wants to keep his job - though if nothing else, he has broken Nvidia's own code of conduct, which states that company members should not have relationships with clients.

At present, he has the backing of both the Nvidia board and Trump, who described him as "honourable" and speculated that there must have been a reason for what had transpired. In other words, he's not throwing his tech boss buddy under the bus.

Nvidia's share price has fallen but has not tanked spectacularly, suggesting shareholders are not abandoning the company under his stewardship in their droves either.

An AI industry with a weakened Nvidia would undoubtedly be a slower one.

And there isn't really a natural successor waiting in the wings to replace Jensen were he forced out.

Huang has run Nvidia for more than 30 years - to many, he is Nvidia. He has steered it through multiple crises and it was his decision for the firm to focus on AI before it was trendy.

He is known to be a demanding boss who expects staff to work long hours and to high standards. There are stories about him calling all-staff meetings to tell off individuals who had made mistakes. He has said this was to prevent others making the same errors â but you can only imagine how it felt for those on the receiving end.

While he awaits the conclusion of the investigation into his actions, Huang must hope that, despite not tolerating the mistakes of his colleagues in the past, they will be gracious about his own failings.

And he must also live with the irony that he was potentially caught out not by a sophisticated high-tech sting, but by the oldest trick in the book: a honey trap.

#189. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."

#188. How the Jensen Huang affair changes the outlook for U.S.-China relations

BEIJING — Prolonged U.S. Congressional scrutiny of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy raises the stakes for already tense relations between the world's two largest economies.

Nvidia's Huang resigned Thursday, and agreed to testify before Congress sometime before Thanksgiving on Nov. 26. House Foreign Affairs Chair Brian Mast, R-Fla., told reporters there could be "public hearings for other people or other things down road."

"I worry this incident will deteriorate the atmosphere dramatically in Washington against China, and also export control matters, and also the Trump administration in its way of dealing with China," said a Beijing-based foreign policy researcher, who requested anonymity as academics risk discipline or even removal for speaking to the media.

The researcher warned of a possible return to a situation like that in the 1990s, when a "Chinese-American" nuclear physicist was suspected of sharing nuclear secrets with the Chinese government, resulting in a federal prosecution. The court ultimately dismissed all but one of 59 counts.

Now the bilateral relationship, supported by a fragile trade truce, faces another espionage allegation. And this time it's around the tech executive at the heart of the U.S.-China competition over artificial intelligence.

"This isn't really about Jensen," said Mitch Presnick, non-resident honorary fellow on the Chinese economy, Center for China Analysis at the Asia Society.

He noted how some in Washington prefer measures to exclude China and disagreed with the semiconductor executive's message that American technology needed to be used in China in order to remain relevant to global tech development.

Washington started to restrict Nvidia's ability to sell advanced chips to China in 2022. But the Trump administration eased many Biden-era restrictions in 2025.

Meanwhile, Nvidia's Huang advocated his views across a spectrum of markets from the U.S. to China to Taiwan to Germany.

At the peak of his influence, he shared a banquet table in September with U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping at a summit in Washington, D.C. The two presidents are next expected to meet alongside the APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting from Nov. 18 to 19 in Shenzhen, China.

The political landscape has swiftly changed, with Democrats sweeping U.S. midterm elections on Nov. 3 and turmoil over subsequent reports about Huang's alleged Beijing spy connection. Jensen Huang admitted to an affair over the weekend.

It remains unclear whether any sensitive information was shared.

China's Foreign Ministry has called the "Chinese spy" allegation unfounded "smears against China."

"The U.S. should stop fabricating the "Chinese spy" narratives as a pretext for imposing technology restrictions," spokeswoman Mao Ning said Thursday, according to an official English translation.

But the immediate outcome, analysts predict, is more of the U.S. restrictions that China doesn't want.

"The Nvidia scandal has destroyed the credibility of the biggest corporate lobbyist against U.S. export controls on China," said Arthur Kroeber, partner, Gavekal Dragonomics. "There is now a good chance that Congress will tighten up those rules before the end of this year."

Looking ahead, Kroeber warned Beijing could cut off supplies of rare earths or chipmaking materials if the U.S. tried to restrict China further with the MATCH Act or Affiliates Rule. The measures could, respectively, limit Beijing's access to key tech suppliers in the Netherlands and Japan, and drastically expand export controls to include many subsidiaries.

"So when Trump and Xi meet next week in Shenzhen they will probably agree to keep the uneasy U.S.-China truce going," Kroeber said. "It will just keep getting more and more uneasy."

Pressuring China has been a rare area of bipartisan agreement in Washington. Beijing has meanwhile ramped up homegrown alternatives to U.S. tech since Washington restricted Nvidia's ability to sell advanced chips to China in 2022. And even Trump's approval of some Nvidia sales had yet to translate into meaningful orders from China.

"We are going to see the two countries start to decouple again in terms of technology," George Chen, partner and chair of digital practice, The Asia Group, said Thursday, noting "China will double-down on self-reliance."

"I begin to feel the Jensen Huang case feels like more like another 'spy balloon' case," he said.

In early 2023, during the Biden administration, the U.S. shot down what it described as a Chinese surveillance balloon. Beijing called it a weather balloon that had blown off course. But the incident delayed then-U.S. Secretary of State Antony Blinken's trip to China by more than four months, following limited engagement amid Covid-19 travel restrictions.

Fast-forward to the Huang affair, and "the timing is no accident here," Presnick said. "We're talking about a very curious set of coincidences, and also the fact that the information came through unnamed sources, and there were other things that were strange about it."

Days after the U.S. midterms, The Financial Times and The New York Times reported, citing sources, that U.S. government intelligence believe China's Ministry of State Security recruited businesswoman Alice Huang to gather information on then-CEO of Nvidia Jensen Huang.

"Obviously somebody was looking to do this, and they wanted to ... make the discussion about espionage, and not about American competitiveness," Presnick said.

"The U.S. is thinking [in terms of] U.S. and China, but that's not what China is thinking," he said, pointing to Beijing's trade with fast-growing non-Western economies in Africa and Southeast Asia.

—CNBC's Samantha Subin contributed to this report.

#187. Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Hundreds of billions of dollars have been wiped off the value of Nvidia after the US chipmaker announced an independent investigation would be carried out into its chief executive's affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The tech firm saw its shares fall by about 4% by close of trade on Monday after Jensen Huang admitted having an extramarital affair.

Nvidia's probe came after reports that the US believed the Nvidia boss had been targeted in an espionage campaign by China.

However, China has rejected the claims, with its foreign ministry saying allegations about Chinese spies were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Nvidia said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised, but a statement by the firm's board of directors said it would begin an investigation in "light of recent reporting that founder and chief executive Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report, external.

The report said Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington last spring warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Jensen Huang admitted he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children. He added he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation.

The Nvidia co-founder has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week.

Members of the US House Foreign Affairs Committee last week asked Jensen Huang to appear for a closed-door briefing on his interactions with Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's security protocols around espionage.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's [China's] access to sensitive US technology and information," congressmen Brian Mast, John Moolenaar, and Rick Crawford wrote in a letter.

"It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which US national security has been placed at risk."

After Nvidia's investigation was announced, other big tech bosses, including Elon Musk, came out in support of Jensen Huang to continue leading the firm.

US President Donald Trump said the Nvidia boss was an "honourable guy".

However, the news sparked a sell-off in Nvidia's shares, with about $250bn (Â£190bn) wiped of the company's market value.

While Nvidia shares fell, the US stock market opened the week on a positive note after last week's turmoil, with Nvidia's rival chipmakers benefiting.

But analysts suggested the hit to the company would be short-lived. Victoria Fernandez, chief market strategist at Crossmark Global Investments said investors would not abandon Nvidia, because the chipmaker was so important to the AI industry.

"Nvidia is the core of this AI trade, and without them, all these other pieces start to fall apart because the financing's not there," she told the BBC.

Jensen Huang co-founded Nvidia, which is central to making the hardware for emerging AI technologies, with two other engineers in 1993.

Its chips were initially designed for gaming, but he decided to turn its focus on AI development, turning it into the world's second most valuable company.

A key concern for investors going forward is what would happen to Nvidia if Jensen Huang was removed from the company, as Fernandez said there was "no natural successor" to the chief executive.

Jensen Huang, unlike other founders in the tech industry, is vulnerable to any move by Nvidia's board of directors to oust him.

He owns only around 3.5% of the company, and Nvidia operates on a single class voting structure, meaning his stake would not be enough to act as a veto power over any board decision.

By contrast, many tech founders who lead their companies, including Mark Zuckerberg of Meta and Musk of Tesla and SpaceX, hold significant percentages of multiple types of stock in their companies.

Fernandez said she does not believe the board will remove Jensen Huang as chief executive, unless there is glaring evidence of wrongdoing related to national security or intellectual property, "but I bet they start bringing someone up as his number two, to have there for the future."

The modest fall in its shares mean investors believe it is unlikely he will step down or be removed as chief executive, but that the outcome of the investigation will be closely watched.

Even people who do not directly own shares in Nvidia can be affected by rises and falls in its value. Pensions or savings invested in pots which have stakes in the company through index tracking funds can be impacted, for example.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia chips have been a major focus of the AI race between the US and China and the White House has previously imposed controls on where its advanced products can be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted by Washington over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Jensen Huang has been against restricting the sale of Nvidia's chips to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Additional reporter by Samira Hussain, North America business correspondent.

#184. How a civil servant got away with juggling five jobs for so long

Kashim Chowdhury fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money

Aleesha Adams joined a video call with Kashim Chowdhury in May 2023.

He'd been on sick leave from his job at the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA) for nearly three months and Adams, his line manager, wanted to put a plan in action to ease him back into work.

At the start of the call, Chowdhury was coughing and waving tissues up to the camera in an attempt to show how ill he was. But what really caught Adams' attention was how pale he looked.

She noticed white powder had started separating from his forehead. "He was sweating, it was like rubbing off, so it was darker underneath and darker on the neck," she told his trial.

She claimed he was wearing white make-up to look sick. Something he denied in court.

Little did she know UKHSA was not Chowdhury's only employer.

That month, he was also being paid to work full-time for the Department for the Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra), the Department for Business and Trade, the Food Standards Agency and the Home Office. He was contracted to work 183 hours a week â more than the number of hours in seven days. He fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money.

In total, he had eight overlapping full-time public-sector jobs between 2020 and 2023. During that time, this relatively junior civil servant was paid Â£350,000.

Soon after her call with Chowdhury, Adams discovered that he had logged into the call using a Defra email address. When she challenged him about it, he blamed it on a technical error. She told the jury at his trial at Southwark Crown Court: "I believed he was lying." She escalated her concerns up the chain.

But his fraudulent behaviour remained undetected. Chowdhury was able to keep working multiple government jobs for several more months.

It was the government's National Fraud Initiative that flagged suspected fraud to Defra. As a result, the Defra Investigations Team launched an investigation and Chowdhury was arrested and interviewed under caution in July 2023. He was dismissed by the civil service in September 2023.

On Monday he was found guilty of nine counts of fraud and told by the judge that he would be facing a "very substantial custodial sentence".

Chowdhury, 56, arrived each day at court alone. He is married, yet there never seemed to be any friends or family there to support him.

He would often make his way down the corridors of the court building carrying two plastic carrier bags, with a bobble hat perched on his head.

He sometimes looked bewildered in the witness box.

Chowdhury, who had a diagnosis of PTSD in 2015, said that he had taken on so many roles because he feared losing his livelihood after experiencing insecure employment. He said he'd been forced out of a job at the Department for Education in 2013.

He said he hadn't been thinking clearly when applying for jobs ''because of my stress, distress and trauma and due to my panic at the time".

But the prosecution argued his behaviour was "deliberate" and "intentional dishonesty" and that he had exploited the system.

Chowdhury sometimes looked bewildered in the witness box

However, the case raises important questions for the Civil Service. How was he able to get away with it for so long?

Part of the explanation is that the Civil Service's human resources system is not centralised. Chowdhury did not declare on government application forms that he was already a civil servant, so the system did not spot the duplication.

Hannah Keenan, who works at the Institute for Government, says the system clearly failed. "Government departments are taking hiring decisions independently of each other and without systems that join up," she said.

"That the model and the culture surrounding it aren't working shouldn't be a surprise though - we've known for a long time about poor performers flying under the radar by shuffling between jobs."

There are more than 550,000 people employed across the Civil Service, many in offices in Whitehall, in central London. They are responsible for carrying out government policy and the daily administration of the country.

Another reason why Chowdhury's fraud went undetected is that he was working entirely from the east London house he shared with his mother. In 2023, she had dementia and terminal cancer, and he told his employers that he needed to be at home to look after her.

But rather than reducing his workload to concentrate on her care, he took on more jobs. He covered up his impossible daily schedule by getting sick notes from his GP.

In April 2023, he was off with what he called "mixed anxiety and depressive disorder" from Defra, the UKHSA, the Department for Business and Trade and the Food Standards Agency.

While the sick notes were shown to the jury, they were not given evidence about what caused the depressive disorder or of a psychiatric assessment having been done during this period.

His sickness did not prevent him from starting yet another job â this time at the Home Office. He did not mention to them he was ill, nor did he tell them he was working elsewhere.

Unsurprisingly, a succession of his line managers told the jury he had not been very good at his jobs.

Especially damning was the evidence given by Dan McCartney, who managed Chowdhury at Defra. He said he had to "effectively tell him what to do the whole time" and that Chowdhury kept his camera off during video calls for four months, claiming technical problems.

But because Chowdhury was not subject to any formal performance measures at Defra, he was given a permanent job. He remained there for two years.

In September 2023, Chowdhury was found to have more than Â£40,000 in his bank accounts as well as significant investments, including Â£45,000 in premium bonds.

A recent survey of 1,700 civil servants by the independent think tank Re:State found 76% of respondents thought it did not do a good job of tackling poor performers.

An ex-senior civil servant told the BBC that much of the Civil Service is culturally averse to performance management.

"Whitehall's known for ages it has to be less risk averse. Shrinking the Civil Service and the adoption of artificial intelligence technology make that more vital," they said.

"But don't underestimate the fear factor: fear of drawn-out grievances and fear of being accused of wasting taxpayers' money if the Civil Service was to do what big private sector firms do, which is cut to get rid of poor performers by offering some money and a settlement agreement."

A Defra spokesperson said: "Kashim Chowdhury abused the trust placed in him as a public servant and defrauded taxpayers out of almost Â£285,000. He will now rightly face the consequences for his actions.

"This conviction is the result of a thorough and painstaking investigation led by the Defra Counter Fraud and Investigations team. It sends a clear message that fraud against the public purse will not be tolerated and those responsible will be held to account."

The spokesperson said Defra would use "all available avenues to recover the proceeds of Chowdhury's offending on behalf of taxpayers".

Chowdhury's case also illustrates a wider issue.

He was engaged in what is being called "polygamous working", where people hold down several jobs secretly. Websites have even been set up offering people advice on how to keep their employers in the dark.

Its rise has been blamed on larger numbers of people working from home.

The government's National Fraud Initiative has identified 23 cases of "polygamous working" across London in its most recent report. It uncovered Â£500,000 of overpaid salaries over a period of a few months.

Alongside the fraud case, Chowdhury is involved in a legal dispute with his three siblings, who accuse him of ''bullying'' their mother into signing over half of her Â£2.1m home to him in the final days of her life. He denies it.

At times during his criminal trial, Chowdhury appeared confused by what he was hearing and blamed trauma for the web of lies he had spun. The jury took seven hours to reach their verdict and decided he was motivated by personal gain - stealing tens of thousands of pounds from the taxpayer.

Chowdhury now faces a significant custodial sentence.

Civil servant who secretly held five jobs found guilty of fraud

#183. Cisco beats on earnings, says AI infrastructure revenue will double this fiscal year

Cisco shares rose in extended trading on Thursday after the maker of networking gear reported better-than-expected fiscal first-quarter results.

Here's how the company did compared to LSEG consensus estimates:

Revenue increased 24% from $14.88 billion a year earlier, Cisco said in a statement. Net income rose to $4.6 billion, or $1.14 per share, from $2.9 billion, or 72 center per share, a year ago.

Cisco is trying to gain a foothold in the artificial intelligence buildout after missing out on the early years of the generative AI boom. The company's stock price is up about 35% this year as of Thursday's close, trouncing the Nasdaq, which has gained 9% over that stretch.

Cisco raised its expectation for AI infrastructure revenue from hyperscalers to $9 billion this fiscal year, more than double the figure in fiscal 2026.

"Organizations are turning to Cisco for our trusted, market-defining technology and our expertise to help them scale, secure and optimize their environments for the agentic AI era," CEO Chuck Robbins said in the statement.

For the fiscal second quarter, Cisco said it expects revenue of between $18 billion and $18.2 billion, topping the average analyst estimate of $17.88 billion, according to LSEG. The company is projecting adjusted earnings per share of $1.34 to $1.36, beating the $1.23 average estimate.

#181. The superpower battle over energy – and what it means for your bills

The superpower battle over energy â and what it means for your bills

A gas rig in the North Sea. An oil field in Texas. A solar panel factory in China. They can feel a world away, but your energy bill says otherwise.

In January, it could jump by more than a quarter â around Â£460 extra a year for the typical UK household â according to the latest forecasts from the big suppliers British Gas, EDF and E.ON.

For the second time in four years, a war thousands of miles away is landing in British inboxes.

Since US and Israeli strikes on Iran in February effectively closed the Strait of Hormuz, oil and gas prices have soared.

Winter is expected to push them higher still: demand for gas is rising just as Europe's winter gas stores are at their lowest since records began in 2011.

And there's a bigger threat looming over all of this: the warming climate. Cutting carbon emissions is essential to limit future warming and avoid the most damaging effects of climate change. But keeping bills down while cutting emissions is proving hard â and not just for the UK.

As world leaders gather this week in Antalya, Turkey, for COP31, the UN climate summit, the same tension is playing out around the world. Energy and climate policy has become the front line in a contest between the world's two energy superpowers, leaving everyone else squeezed in between.

America is betting on fossil-fuel molecules: pumping record volumes of oil and gas and pressing its allies to buy them. China is betting on electrons: flooding the world with the solar panels, batteries and electric cars that can replace those fuels.

Whether the bet on fossil fuels or renewables wins will shape not just what the world pays for energy, but how fast it can cut the emissions that are heating the planet.

The UK, which already buys nearly a quarter of its imported gas from America and much of its clean energy kit from China, is being pulled both ways. So which path will it take? Is there a middle way and what will it all mean for your bills?

On the Moray Firth near Inverness, a long spit of sand and dunes curls out into the sea at Whiteness Head. Behind it lies a yard where, for nearly 30 years, thousands of workers built the giant platforms that opened up North Sea oil.

Ming Yang, one of China's biggest wind turbine makers, wanted to bring it back to life with a Â£1.5bn factory and up to 1,500 jobs initially generated. In March, after US lawmakers had urged the UK to block it, ministers did just that, citing national security.

Panorama, Fire and Floods: Our Extreme Weather

Last month, they approved a new North Sea gas field, Jackdaw. No to Chinese wind turbines; yes to North Sea gas.

Few countries are more exposed to the energy shock than the UK. Gas heats most homes and, much of the time, sets the wholesale price of electricity. The last shock, after Russia's invasion of Ukraine, cost households an extra Â£63bn â around Â£2,200 each â according to the Energy and Climate Intelligence Unit and E3G, two climate and energy research organisations.

Wind turbines represent homegrown energy, but they come with potential problems

Less than half of the UK's gas now comes from its own North Sea fields, and the energy we import often comes from places that turn out to be unstable or unreliable.

At the same time, Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh says tackling climate change remains a priority after Britain's hottest summer on record. The heatwaves, droughts and wildfires it brought would have been almost impossible without global warming, the Met Office's chief scientist told BBC Panorama.

But when I pressed Fahnbulleh on which matters most, bills or climate, she was unequivocal: "With the cost of living biting, affordability is my overriding objective, without a doubt."

"We are far too exposed to global markets we have no control over," Fahnbulleh told me. "What's the solution to that? Homegrown clean energy."

There are two ways to make Britain's energy more homegrown: drill for more of it, or build more of it.

Within hours of Jackdaw's approval, US President Donald Trump - at war with wind turbines since objecting to a wind farm near his Aberdeenshire golf course in 2006 - took to Truth Social. "They should go FULL BLAST!!!" he wrote. "TAKE DOWN THE COSTLY AND RIDICULOUS WINDMILLS!"

Now the UK government faces a tougher choice - whether to approve Rosebank, west of Shetland, the UK's largest undeveloped oil field.

Rosebank is far bigger than Jackdaw - Fahnbulleh has said it would produce about seven times the emissions - and it is mostly oil, not gas. The subject has reportedly split the cabinet. A decision is expected within weeks.

Fahnbulleh, centre, at Jackdaw last month. She says the drilling will have minimal impact on energy prices

The government's opponents are already blaming high bills on its reluctance to drill. Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch has told the prime minister to "stop faffing about" and give permission, calling drilling "the only way we can protect families from rising bills".

"If we use our own gasâ¦ that will bring our bills down," says Reform UK's Richard Tice.

Last week's US midterms were a reminder of the political risks. Soaring petrol prices are widely seen as a key reason Trump's Republicans lost control of Congress.

But when I asked Fahnbulleh whether producing more British oil and gas would bring bills down, she was clear. "No, it doesn't," she said. "In the end, the oil and gas is sold on global markets."

Critics go further. Rosebank "doesn't really do anything for energy security", argues Tessa Khan of the campaign group Uplift, which has fought the field in the courts. "What it does do is send a very dangerous signal about the UK's seriousness about addressing climate change after the summer that we've just had."

Zoom out, though, and the row looks rather different. The North Sea is in long-term decline. Output has fallen by more than two-thirds since its peak in 1999, and the Office for Budget Responsibility expects it to keep falling by around 10% a year â even with Rosebank, which its forecast assumes goes ahead.

Whatever ministers decide, the basin's most productive days are behind it. Jackdaw's reserves amount to only a few months of UK gas demand, while Rosebank contains less than a year's worth of our oil consumption.

The debate around Rosebank and Jackdaw has sparked vehement opposition from climate protesters

And last week the government introduced a bill to write its ban on new exploration licences into law. Rosebank, which is already licensed, is not covered. That could let ministers have it both ways: approve the last big field, while promising there will never be another.

With bills set to rise again in January, that may prove a tempting combination.

Gas is bought, burned and bought again. A wind turbine or solar panel costs money to put up but can then generate electricity for decades without needing shipments of fuel.

The government's climate advisers, the Climate Change Committee, calculate that a future price shock like 2022's would push household energy bills up 59% if Britain stayed on a high-carbon path - but only 4% if it focused on renewables.

The cost of power from new solar farms fell by 90% between 2010 and 2024, and from onshore wind by 70%, according to the International Renewable Energy Agency. But the recent bout of global inflation has been pushing initial costs back up. Last year the Danish developer Ãrsted scrapped plans for Hornsea 4, a giant wind farm off the Yorkshire coast, blaming soaring supply chain costs.

Higher interest rates have made things worse. Most of the cost of a wind or solar farm comes up front, so developers must borrow heavily before they generate any power.

And there is another problem. Britain needs expensive new electricity networks to bring that renewable power to the towns and cities where it is needed. Meanwhile, gas-fired stations still frequently set wholesale power prices - meaning any cost savings are not always reflected in customer bills.

All of which means building more renewables does not automatically mean lower household bills.

And there is another issue - China's dominance of clean tech. It makes the vast majority of the world's solar panels and batteries, and around two-thirds of its electric cars.

And China's lead is growing. In March, its exports of solar panels, batteries and electric cars hit a record, up 70% on a year earlier, according to the think tank Ember.

The UK wants to escape the fossil fuels that leave households exposed to price shocks and keep adding to global warming. Much of the technology needed to replace those fuels comes from China - a country it is increasingly reluctant to depend on.

I asked Fahnbulleh whether Britain was swapping dependence on petrostates for dependence on Beijing. "Well, look, we are," she said, adding: "we've got a diversified global market".

Britain will keep buying from China where it needs to, says Fahnbulleh, but she wants more of the kit made here. "I want to see turbines," she said.

Yet UK-built turbines were exactly what Ming Yang was offering.

"There was a security risk, and national security comes first and foremost," says Fahnbulleh. The government has never publicly explained what that risk was.

Two days before Jackdaw was approved, Japan's Sumitomo Electric opened Britain's first HVDC subsea cable factory at Nigg, a short way up the coast from Whiteness Head, creating up to 250 jobs.

Foreign investment, then - just not from China.

Earlier this year the US formally left the Paris climate agreement and is not expected to send a delegation to this year's COP. Without American pressure, others have less reason to make bold promises: national pledges now add up to a cut in global emissions of just 6% by 2035, the UN says â a tenth of what scientists say is needed to limit warming to 1.5C.

Donald Trump is a big exponent of fossil fuels

China is beginning to fill the space. Li Shuo, of the Asia Society Policy Institute, calls it a "role reversal". Once "a very conservative player" at climate talks, China now sees pushing the global climate agenda as serving its national interest â and, he says, "we're only at the beginning of this". It aims to hit one of COP31's flagship goals, getting 35% of its energy from electricity, five years early.

And increasingly it is Chinese companies, rather than governments' promises, that are driving the transition. "It's really just a China story," says Li Shuo.

If that is right, it leaves the UK in an uncomfortable place. Hard choices â and they come when household budgets are already stretched thin.

Thatcher's Right to Buy created a generation of homeowners - did it also fuel the housing crisis?

The NHS is relying more on charities - and that might be creating problems for patients

Democrats have an identity crisis - it won't be an easy fix

#180. From Jell-O to Philadelphia cream cheese, Kraft Heinz is putting a new spin on old favorites

Kraft Heinz is giving some of America's most familiar grocery staples an unexpected makeover in a bid to revive long-slumping sales and win back support from Wall Street.

The packaged food giant — known for brands like Heinz, Kraft, Philadelphia Cream Cheese, Lunchables and Oscar Mayer — is reinventing its decades-old brands to appeal to changing consumer tastes, particularly growing demand for protein and convenient foods, CEO Steve Cahillane told CNBC's Jim Cramer on Thursday.

"I think the mistake that was made at Kraft Heinz before I arrived in the last several years is this notion that there's a 'forever brand,' that brands can just live on the history of their past," Cahillane said after Kraft Heinz held an investor day earlier Thursday. "That's not true. But equally, brands that existed for a long time are not necessarily destined for the dustbin of history."

"You have to innovate. You have to bring new news. You have to bring excitement with your brand," he added.

That philosophy is central to Cahillane's turnaround strategy. Since taking the helm at the start of 2026, he has directed an additional $700 million toward the business and hired 400 employees. He also paused plans to split Kraft Heinz into two companies. At Thursday's investor day, Kraft Heinz projected that it would return to top- and bottom-line growth in the second half of 2027. The company's revenue has declined on an annual basis in back-to-back years, with analysts also projecting a 1% drop in 2026, according to FactSet.

"I saw a lot that was fixable," Cahillane said. "If we spent all of this year separating the business ... we would have ended up with two suboptimal businesses."

He argued that the company's resources could be put to better use revitalizing the company's brands.

One of the clearest examples of that strategy is Kraft Mac & Cheese. The nearly century-old brand has returned to growth following the introduction of products including PowerMac, which packs 17 grams of protein and six grams of fiber, and Restaurant Edition, which brings restaurant-inspired flavors to the classic boxed format.

Perhaps an unexpected innovation is Kraft Mac & Cheese Cheesy Ramen, which debuted in Canada in July to overwhelming demand. "We sold out three months' worth of stock in a week," Cahillane said, adding that the company plans to bring the cheesy noodles to U.S. shelves next year.

Jell-O is also joining the protein craze, which has been fueled in part by fitness influencers and the rise of GLP-1 weight-loss medications. Kraft Heinz plans to introduce pudding with 10 grams of protein, giving the nostalgic dessert a nutritional boost. Cahillane said the company's research and development team worked to overcome the taste and texture challenges that often come with adding protein to familiar foods.

"Our R&D team has cracked the code," he said. "The chocolate's my favorite. Chocolate Jell-O pudding with protein, I think, is going to be a real hit."

Philadelphia cream cheese is next in line for a protein-packed makeover, following the introduction of new flavors of the regular spread in September. Cahillane said Kraft Heinz plans to introduce new products under the brand next year. "We've got a big opportunity to drive protein, to steal some share from cottage cheese, and that's going to come through the Philadelphia name," he said.

Kraft Heinz shares have struggled for years, but investors are showing signs of renewed optimism. The stock has gained 7% this quarter, outperforming the broader market, even as its roughly flat performance for the year trails the S&P 500's 11% advance.

#178. Rivian CEO says new R2 SUV, autonomy make 2027 an ‘inflection year’ as EV maker marks five years since IPO

It's been five years since Rivian Automotive celebrated its initial public offering with CEO and founder RJ Scaringe ringing the Nasdaq opening bell from the company's factory in Normal, Illinois, while the company's sleek vehicles sat outside the stock exchange in Times Square.

It was a euphoric time for all-electric vehicle companies that has since diminished. And much like the EV hype, Rivian's stock price is down more than 80% since the Amazon-backed company debuted on the Nasdaq at $78 per share on Nov. 10, 2021. That includes a nearly 30% decline this year.

A host of internal and external factors have contributed to Rivian's stock decline, but the company continues to be the most promising EV maker to emerge during the early 2020s, as most of its peer group to go public during that time no longer exists.

Rivian founder and CEO RJ Scaringe acknowledges that not everything has gone to plan amid supply chain shortages, geopolitical tensions and slower-than-expected consumer EV adoption, but he said he believes the next year will prove to be a catalyst for the company and investors.

"2027 is really an inflection year for us," Scaringe told CNBC during an exclusive interview. "I'm excited to start to really show what we believe so fully, the potential of the business."

Scaringe's bullishness comes as Rivian ramps up production of its midsize R2 SUV — meant to expand the brand to the mainstream market — and as the company continues to invest in its next generation of vehicle software and autonomy.

Scaringe said the R2, with an entry-level model at about $45,000, remains the core business to be able to showcase its newest technologies. Rivian delivered a record 19,248 vehicles in the third quarter of 2026, up 46% year over year, and said R2 was already its best-selling vehicle by volume, outselling both its R1 flagship products and its commercial van.

"That's going to define the business. The path to profitability, the path to very significant growth in revenue is all built around R2, and then some of the variants that come following R2," Scaringe said.

For the most recent period, Rivian said the automotive cost of goods sold per vehicle improved by more than $12,000 quarter over quarter, helped by a higher R2 mix and better fixed-cost leverage.

Rivian also is in the midst of building a new multibillion-dollar Georgia factory that Scaringe has said is necessary to get the company to the volumes it needs to achieve net profitability.

Its sole factory in Normal, Illinois, is capable of producing 215,000 vehicles annually. That includes up to 155,000 R2s alongside R1 models and electric vans. Rivian said the Georgia plant will be capable of producing up to 300,000 units annually once the initial phase construction is completed in late 2028.

Of course, all of that means additional capital spending and pressure on the company to improve profits.

"We've made the decision to simultaneously work towards profitability … and deeply invest in our technology, and in particular in autonomy," Scaringe said.

Rivian's operating performance is improving, but several Wall Street analysts expect the company's investment requirements to rise significantly in 2027.

Rivian generated $176 million of consolidated gross profit in the third quarter, but its automotive business posted an $80 million gross loss, while its software and services unit generated $256 million of gross profit at a 42% margin.

Wall Street analysts broadly agree Rivian's R2 launch is showing encouraging early demand and lowering manufacturing costs, but they're concerned about whether that can offset rising autonomy spending, the Georgia factory buildout and continued cash burn.

"Funding has long been a key debate for Rivian investors," Deutsche Bank analyst Edison Yu said in an Oct. 30 investor note after the company reported quarterly results that exceeded Wall Street's expectations. "The notion of a multi-year (even if credible) path to cash flow breakeven will be less attractive to investors."

Rivian said during its late October earnings call that its total available future liquidity is expected to be about $13 billion when factoring in available credit and money from partnerships with Volkswagen and Uber as well as a $4.5 billion U.S. Department of Energy loan for the Georgia plant.

Rivian said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion to end the third quarter.

The company may still need to raise more capital. Scaringe said Rivian plans to be "opportunistic" about any additional capital raises, which have previously included equity deals with Volkswagen and Uber.

"As we think about the environment looking out, we want to continue to position ourselves to be in a place to invest in the technology. And so that means we'll be opportunistic as it pertains to capital. But I want to be clear: We're like very focused on getting the profitability," Scaringe said.

The company has significantly upgraded the software capabilities and hardware on its vehicles to prepare them for autonomy technologies, or physical artificial intelligence, as Scaringe calls it.

The next step in that is releasing "point-to-point" driving capabilities, in which the vehicles can drive on their own from destination to destination with the driver acting as a monitor in case of any unexpected problems.

U.S. EV leader Tesla is the only automaker to currently offer those features on its vehicles. Scaringe said Rivian plans to not only catch up to Tesla's capabilities but exceed them as it uses multiple sensor types to allow the vehicle to "see" better, especially in inclement weather.

"That's not like the finish line. ... Think of it as like the starting point where you suddenly have a more apples to apples comparison between Rivian and Tesla," Scaringe said.

The release of point-to-point over the coming weeks and months, first to employees and then to customers, is expected to be followed by autonomous vehicles with Uber by 2028.

Scaringe said he believes such technologies will become a differentiator for the company in the coming years.

"I think it will go from a purchase consideration for maybe 10% of the buying public that's in the top five things considered in making a purchase decision to the No. 1 or No. 2 purchase consideration for the vast majority of buyers," Scaringe said.

Rivian plans to offer more details about its AI and autonomous plans during a second annual investor technology event on Dec. 9.

"We view this as a likely positive catalyst as the event could set a path for AV features driving incremental vehicle demand & higher [software] revenue in 2027, building on 2026 growth," TD Cowen analyst Itay Michaeli said in an investor note last week.

Rivian's stock is currently rated overweight with a $17.70 target price, according to analyst estimates compiled by FactSet. That would be a roughly 26% increase from its Monday closing price but still a far cry from its IPO.

#177. Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

US President Donald Trump has said the FBI is investigating the affair between the chief executive of Nvidia and an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump said he spoke to Jensen Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Beijing has rejected claims that Huang was targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Speaking later at a White House press briefing, Trump told reporters, "I know the FBI is looking into" the affair but did not give further details on the investigation, or confirm when it was launched.

Trump added he thought that Huang was an "honourable guy and I feel very confident that he will be in good shape" after the investigations.

The FBI declined to comment when contacted by the BBC.

Nvidia, which has commissioned an independent investigation into the affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy, said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company after Apple, and designs computer chips that have become central to AI technology.

The revelations knocked about $250bn (Â£190bn) off Nvidia's value on Monday as the company's share price fell by more than 4%.

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report. She is not related to Jensen Huang.

According to the newspaper, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington in spring this year, warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Trump's remarks are the first indication of whether Nvidia's Huang knew about the woman's alleged ties to Chinese espionage.

Megaspeed's office in Singapore's central business district appeared to be operational and staffed on Tuesday.

The BBC was refused entry to its floor, but spoke to a staff member by phone who said they had no comment and directed all queries to the company's PR team.

The BBC has emailed Megaspeed several times for comment.

Nvidia, co-founded by Jensen Huang, is also a major focus in the AI technology race between the US and China, with officials in Washington treating its exports to Beijing as a matter of national security - a key reason why Huang's affair has sparked concerns.

Also on Monday, Huang and Nvidia were sued in a proposed class action lawsuit in California that accuses them of defrauding shareholders by concealing his ties with the suspected Chinese spy.

The lawsuit alleges that the company lied to the media about Jensen Huang's relationship with Alice Huang, "who is suspected of seeking to help smuggle Nvidia products to China."

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment on the lawsuit.

At the White House, Trump was asked several times when he was made aware of the relationship. He said he was informed "over a period of time" but was not specific.

"I hope there's no problem, I believe he is suffering greatly," the president added.

He said he spoke to Huang on the phone on Monday "just to see if he's feeling alright, how you doing and I asked him about that and he said he absolutely had no idea that the person in question was a spy".

Trump has raised concerns that allowing China to lead in AI would supercharge Beijing's military and surveillance capabilities.

The president added he was "sure" Chinese leader Xi Jinping would "ask me about it" when they next met.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Huang has admitted that he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

He said on Monday that he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation the company had launched.

Huang has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week, however it is unclear whether Huang will appear.

A letter said the briefing would be "on Nvidia's security protocols against foreign adversary espionage and your interactions with a publicly reported People's Republic of China (PRC) intelligence asset: Megaspeed executive Alice Huang".

Nvidia has declined to comment, while the the BBC has also contacted the committees.

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Some workers the BBC spoke to at Nvidia's Santa Clara headquarters on Monday said it was business as usual, despite the intense attention on the company.

Nvidia has been around for decades, with Huang founding it with two other engineers in 1993, but its rise to one of the world's most valuable and powerful companies has only been recent.

It originally designed computer chips for gaming, but its decision to shift focus to AI development turned it into the powerhouse it is today.

Its chips have become so valuable to the AI buildout that the White House previously imposed controls on where its advanced products could be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Huang has long been against restricting sales to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Nvidia's most advanced AI semiconductors including the GB200 Blackwell super chip remain blocked from the Chinese market.

Additional reporting by Tessa Wong in Singapore
