#142. JetBlue unveils new first-class seats to bring back profits

JetBlue Airways unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country's carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. The carrier showed off the new cabin at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport on Wednesday. Retrofits will continue during 2027, CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

JetBlue will have 12 of the seats on its Airbus A320 fleet and 30 extra-legroom seats, down from 42 of those options previously. Because the airplanes don't have ovens, JetBlue said it is offering an array of cold food options like sesame noodles, pastrami sandwiches and tortellini salad, though they vary by route.

The rollout comes as the New York airline tries to stabilize after last posting a profit in 2019. Last month, JetBlue executives sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors.

"We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," Geraghty told CNBC in an interview on Oct. 27.

Geraghty told CNBC's Phil LeBeau on Wednesday that JetBlue has "met with a few of the creditors over the last couple of weeks as part of ... normal investor engagement." She said the company made it clear to bondholders that JetBlue doesn't need to reorganize.

The airline's shares are up more than 40% this month, more than other passenger carriers, as of midday Wednesday, though they are still off about 11% so far this year.

JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

Airline executives have said they expect fares to stay elevated next year as they grapple with higher costs, including but not limited to fuel, such as labor and airport fees. Geraghty said Wednesday that even if fuel prices moderate next year, "I don't think fares are going to fall as much as people expect."

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new recliner first-class seats between Kennedy airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines unveiled its new first-class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seating that has extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview Monday.

JetBlue last month reported that it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty last month said "absolutely not."

#141. Jensen Huang scandal casts doubt on Nvidia’s AI dominance — how we got here

Nvidia is facing one the biggest crises in its 33 year history, with founder Jensen Huang resigning as CEO Thursday after multiple reported affairs, including one with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy. Now CFO Colette Kress has taken the helm as Nvidia's interim CEO.

The implications of Huang's affair with Alice Huang run far deeper than infidelity. That's because her ties to Nvidia customer Megaspeed put her in the middle of a sprawling effort to accumulate AI chips across Southeast Asia. And Megaspeed is under investigation for possibly diverting restricted Nvidia chips into the hands of Chinese companies.

The relationship between Jensen Huang and Alice Huang dates back to 2023, according to The New York Times. That was a "turbulent" period for Nvidia, Jensen Huang told CNBC during an interview at the time. Nvidia was in the midst of redesigning some chips to comply with the first in a series of strict export controls imposed by the U.S. government that restricted sales to China.

"The regulation is going to be just fine. We're going to be able to work through it," Huang told CNBC from Nvidia's headquarters in February 2023. The interview was for a documentary about how Huang created Nvidia and built it into the backbone of the AI boom.

That same day, CNBC also interviewed Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare, with whom Jensen Huang reportedly had an affair.

It's not the first time Nvidia has faced an uncertain future. The company nearly went bankrupt in the early years after it was founded in 1993.

"Every company makes mistakes and I make a lot of them," Huang said in the 2023 interview. "And some of them put the company in peril."

In the early months of the AI boom, after OpenAI launched ChatGPT in November 2022, Nvidia was on the precipice of historic growth. Jensen Huang told CNBC he'd like to keep running the company for "three or four decades," as long as "I feel that I'm making a real contribution to the company."

Less than four years later, Jensen Huang is out, and we're taking a look back at our interviews with the Nvidia co-founder and other company executives as the chipmaker enters this next critical chapter. Watch the video for the full story.

#139. Poundland gets more time to secure a rescue deal as emergency talks continue

Poundland has avoided immediate collapse after a deadline was extended to find a rescue deal.

A deadline to secure a buyer for the retail chain was set to expire at midnight, but a court has given Poundland an extra 10 days to find a buyer.

The discount chain, which has 11,000 employees, has been edging towards administration after being blocked by owners Gordon Brothers from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

A committee of MPs questioned Gordon Brothers following accusations that the investment firm hoped to profit from selling off Poundland's 600 shops and stock piecemeal.

Gordon Brothers - which bought the chain for just Â£1 last year - is accused of forcing that outcome by restricting Poundland's access to available cash needed to restock shops, and pay wages and rent, ahead of the crucial Christmas trading period.

The investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claims.

However, pressure remains on Poundland's owners after a takeover bid was rejected. The decision prompted calls for answers from politicians, including Prime Minister Andy Burnham.

About Â£50m had been frozen by Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning Poundland bosses were unable to restock shelves across its 600 shops.

The BBC understands that Gordon Brothers agreed to release a further Â£11m today from Poundland's accounts, on top of Â£10m announced earlier, as the firm seeks to find a new buyer.

Gordon Brothers said it was in talks with three parties for a sale of the whole business, and it had received "several offers from established high street retailers for significant parts of the business".

Following the meeting between MPs on the business and trade committee and representatives of Gordon Brothers, chairman Liam Byrne told the BBC that he was "deeply concerned" and described the firm's behaviour as "much like a wolf on Wall Street, on the British High Street".

"They refused to prioritise protecting jobs and stores over and above their own financial returns," he said.

"It's clear that they've taken millions out of Poundland at a time when they themselves said the business was on a knife edge."

Byrne said the committee's message to Gordon Brothers was to ensure Poundland had "access to the funds that it needs to trade at what should be one of the busiest periods in the year".

He urged Gordon Brothers to "do what is right to save jobs and stores and do that now".

"It's a good business, it's a much-loved business and, particularly in the middle of the cost of living crisis, communities up and down the country rely on access to the kind of stores that it operates," Byrne added.

Views on the chain's performance differ between those running operations and the owners.

Poundland boss Williams told staff last week that like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4%. The sales period included Halloween, but Williams said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He added that the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year when there were losses of Â£68m.

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It has argued that the best way of securing jobs may be to split up the business and sell chunks of its 600 sites to other retailers.

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up Poundland shops, which could mean continuing employment for workers at those sites.

The main issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland as a national chain.

#136. UK economy grows at fastest rate for more than two years

Computer programming and consultancy services in particular contributed to overall growth (generic photo)

ï»¿The UK economy grew at its fastest rate for more than two years in the three months to September.

Between July and September, the economy expanded by 0.7%, accelerating from the previous three months, official figures, external show.

The robust growth suggests a far more resilient economy than expected over the summer, despite the impact of higher energy prices from the Iran war.

Strong growth in the services sector - the largest part of the economy, which includes non-physical goods such as consultancy services and computer programming as well as hotels and restaurants - helped boost the numbers.

Within services, computer programming registered its fastest growth for the past three years, helped by cloud computing and AI-linked spending.

The boost from computer programming and consultancy services has been the biggest driver of growth so far this year.

Grant Fitzner, chief economist at the Office for National Statistics, said it was "possible growth could have been even stronger" if there hadn't been a war in the Middle East.

But he says households may still not feel better off, and that prices might soon start to rise more quickly than wages.

However, Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the figures were "welcome news".

"Despite a year where events around the world have pushed up costs here at home, our economy is growing, bringing hope for the future," he said.

Chancellor John Healey told the BBC the figures showed a "remarkable resilience" in the face of global conflicts, and hopes they "bring back confidence in Britain".

Asked about how government decisions could be the reason for growth when AI investment has been a main driver, Healey said ministers can always "help or hinder" business confidence and investments.

Although investors have recently been concerned about slower artificial intelligence development in the US and the long term viability of AI firms' business models, businesses have continued to invest in the technologies.

Sarah Coles, from investment platform AJ Bell, said that since the start of the year, "it's the less visible back-office work that has delivered, with administrative and supportive services seeing strong growth" noting that these are the areas benefitting from the increasing use of AI.

The latest figures put the UK on course to be the second fastest growing economy across the major G7 countries which also includes Canada, Japan, Germany, Italy and France.

However, Jake Finney, senior economist at consultancy PwC, cautioned that growth was likely to slow over the coming months "unless energy markets stabilise".

A sharp rise in energy bills is forecast for January, which is likely to hit consumer spending and confidence, he said.

High energy costs have been stoked by the ongoing conflict in the Middle East and Russia's invasion of Ukraine.

US and Israeli attacks led to Iran blocking the Strait of Hormuz - a vital shipping route for the world's oil and gas.

Meanwhile, Russia and Ukraine have been attacking each other's oil refineries, leading to a squeeze on global refining capacity, hitting diesel and petrol prices.

What's happening to the UK economy and how does it affect you?

Entrepreneur Richard Benc says the UK watch market and exports have been surprisingly resilient

Richard Benc, founder of mechanical watch company Studio Underdog, says sales are better than he expected.

"We've seen more resilience than we had forecast for and expected. We are still projecting growth this year," he says.

The business exports about 80% of its watches. "We've seen a surprising amount of resilience, particularly from our US customers," he adds, although some UK customers are now taking longer to buy watches.

#135. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.

#134. Huang's downfall may undo cozy ties between business and government: analysis

The resignation of Jensen Huang, together with the results of the recent congressional election, could make this a watershed moment in the relationship of American business to government, one that sees the pendulum swing back toward greater regulation and greater separation of finance from politics.

Corruption topped the list among issues that motivated voters in the overwhelming and nationwide midterm election victory for Democrats, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. It showed 90% identified corruption as a crucial or major issue in their voting decision, a percentage point higher than the cost of living.

When voters were asked which party was best able to deal with the corruption issue, Democrats led Republicans 37% to 30% — though, significantly, 32% said neither party.

It's impossible to know exactly what voters mean when they say corruption, but there are hints it involves the relationship of business to government. The CNBC All-America Economic Survey, taken two weeks before the election, asked respondents if elected officials, including the president and members of Congress, should personally own or trade stocks in companies that have contracts with the federal government. An overwhelming 93% said it should not be allowed. A similarly bipartisan 89% objected to the president's family members receiving government contracts.

The upshot: The very close relationship between business and the government, which has existed under both political parties but has come plainly and brazenly to the fore in President Trump's second administration, is now a major issue for voters.

The Huang affair encompasses all of that. Nvidia, along with several other large corporations, made large donations to the president's ballroom. Meanwhile, there are allegations that the administration did not act when informed of potentially illegal sales of Nvidia chips to China. It may be that the president ignored warnings from inside his own administration that Huang was involved with a woman who was reportedly a potential Chinese spy, instead bringing Huang on a state visit to China and giving him say in U.S. export policies for chips.

The Trump era has seen the virtual creation of the CEO Statesman: Huang and other tech execs draped around the president during foreign trips, Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff negotiating with Russia, Elon Musk deciding who the federal government fires.

Could the Huang affair be the end of that phenomenon?

Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., wasted no time in signaling the new Congress was ready to get tough on tech execs.

"It's clear that the American people cannot rely on Big Tech billionaire CEOs to put our national security above their bottom line – which is why Congress must pass the AI Overwatch Act and other bipartisan bills to keep advanced American AI technology out of China's hands."

The enormity of Huang's fall cannot be overstated. Imagine J.P. Morgan resigning — or Jack Welch or Steve Jobs or Tim Cook. Few have stood astride the pinnacle of political and corporate power during their era like Huang during the AI boom.

This comes at a time of massive wealth increases for the elite that have helped undermine faith in capitalism. The CNBC Voter Poll showed 56% with a strong or somewhat favorable opinion of capitalism, with 32% reporting an unfavorable opinion and 12% saying they don't know.

But those numbers are overwhelmingly driven by older Americans. Younger Americans, ages 18 to 34, have a deeply negative opinion. Those aged 35 to 49 are favorable toward capitalism by only a 46%-41% margin.

For starters, it could result in a return to more traditional separation of business and politics, where business has less of a direct say in policy. But this could be the start of a more significant change.

In the wake of the Great Financial Crisis, the banking system was put under such strict regulations, many of which exist to this day, that one commentator remarked it was "in receivership in everything but name only." One can go back even further to more extreme measures taken when the public was deeply dissatisfied with business — to the fallout from the robber baron era when the trusts were busted up, or the wake of the Great Depression when the government put stricter regulations on banks and Wall Street and gave unions more power.

Democrats will be limited, even with large gains in both houses, in their ability to overturn presidential decisions with veto-proof majorities. But executives themselves may decide that it's not a good look any longer to be seen being too cozy with the White House. And some Republicans, especially those from swing states, may read the change in the electoral winds and side with Democrats on some of the most populist issues, like the data centers, the environment and even regulation.

The irony is that Trump ran, among other things, on a strong deregulatory agenda, one that business welcomed as unshackling overregulation during the Biden administration. With potential support from the public, and an overwhelmingly Democratic Congress, the pendulum may be about to swing the other way.

#131. Faisal Islam: Why the UK economy is surprisingly resilient

The UK economy grew at its fastest rate for more than two years in the three months to September.

"Top gear" and "strikingly strong" were among the descriptions of the 0.7% growth offered by the economists who track every minor murmur of Britain's economy.

It is an upgrade on "solid" and "resilient", and in turn a world away from "hardest hit by the Iran War".

Against the backdrop of the world's biggest disruption to energy flows, rising market borrowing costs - and the cruel summers since the pandemic - it is impressive growth.

It should reset some of the swirl of self-reinforcing negativity.

The UK's economic growth has averaged less than 0.1% in the four Q3s [ie July to September] since the pandemic.

The average prediction just months ago for this July to September was just 0.1%.

Even the UK's official forecaster the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR) effectively pencilled in 0.3% at the Budget just 3 weeks ago.

This figure has not been exceeded outside the pandemic since 2017. It puts the UK on course to be the second fastest growing economy after the US across the major G7 countries which also includes Canada, Japan, Germany, Italy and France.

It is tempting to think about what the economy would have done if the Iran War hadn't happened. If interest rates had been cut instead of their recent rise.

While the latest figure kills the doom, it is clearly not a boom either.

Spending on AI and tech have driven the surprise upside over the year. While the AI data centre boom may at any point turn to bust, these figures suggest that spending across the economy on investing in these technologies is helping to boost growth. Consumers have been resilient, at least till the autumn, perhaps helped by better economic vibes.

While there has been an understandable focus on high UK borrowing costs, another measure of the international standing of the UK economy, sterling's value, reached post Brexit referendum highs last week.

Productivity and investment is also looking stronger, especially after recent revisions. Some of the most encouraging figures are in private sector business investment.

I spoke to the chancellor at Nexeon in Oxfordshire, which is searching for a UK factory to manufacture its cutting edge electric vehicle battery - and British - technology. My colleagues visited British watchmakers in Maidenhead carving out a niche to take on the Swiss.

However, this may well not last for the final three months of the year.

Consumer confidence has already fallen as petrol and domestic energy prices start to rise again.

There is a significant challenge with everyday cost-of-living pressures in the coming months. Construction and private house building remain depressed.

The Iran conflict remains the big unknown and whether post the US midterm elections there could be a durable end to the conflict in the Gulf.

Some oil and gas production there is starting to normalise. It could bring down petrol prices, energy bills, government borrowing costs, and mortgage costs too.

Expect to see Downing Street investing heavily in "settlement of the conflict", which the chancellor told me is the "best economic policy".

#130. Nvidia stock inches higher as investors continue to weigh Jensen Huang’s departure

Nvidia shares were slightly higher in premarket trading on Friday as investors navigate the fallout from Jensen Huang's resignation after reports of at least two affairs.

It follows a wild day for the stock on Thursday, which dropped sharply before recovering to close just over 4% lower.

Huang resigned as CEO of Nvidia on Thursday after The New York Times reported that Huang had a romantic relationship with Kimberly Powell, vice president of health care at Nvidia.

Huang was already under scrutiny following an investigation by the board into his relationship with a businesswoman who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy.

Reuters reported on Thursday the existence of another affair with an Nvidia employee, which the news agency did not name. Nvidia did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Shares of Nvidia were 1.7% higher as of 8:13 a.m. ET.

Before Huang resigned, several Wall Street analysts told CNBC that his departure could cost Nvidia as much as 20% of its market value, given how critical he has been to the company's success. Nvidia's stock is already down more than 20% over the last month.

A lot of the company's success has been built around Huang, said Stephane Houri, head of equity research at ODDO BHF.

It's about more than just Nvidia, but also about the "architecture of the AI industry that really depended on the vision" and expertise of Huang, Houri told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Friday.

#129. Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

#126. Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

Arm's boss Rene Haas (L), Nvidia's Colette Kress (C) and Chris Malachowsky (R) have been named as possible successors

Nvidia is now facing a scenario few would have considered until very recently - a future without Jensen Huang.

The influential co-founder of the AI chip-making giant resigned on Thursday over reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Chief financial officer Colette Kress is in charge, for now, as the tech giant faces one of its biggest challenges - finding the right person to permanently lead the business that Huang helped create more than three decades ago and turned into one of the world's most valuable companies.

We take a look at which candidates have emerged as potential successors.

Colette Kress is chief financial officer at Nvidia

Colette Kress has been an industry veteran for more than 30 years, having previously held high positions at tech firms like Cisco and Microsoft.

Kress, who also serves as Nvidia's executive vice-president, has seen the firm's transformation from making chips for video games in the early 1990s to becoming an AI semiconductor powerhouse.

She has "fronted every earnings call" alongside Huang and is a "familiar and trusted voice for the market at a time when it badly needs one," said Josh Gilbert, an analyst at investment firm eToro.

But doing the job permanently could be a different story, said Gilbert, as the firm, known for its edge in technology, could want an engineer in the chair.

Chris Malachowsky is one of Nvidia's three co-founders

Another option is Chris Malachowsky, who founded Nvidia alongside Huang and Curtis Priem, and is the only executive that shares the same level of seniority.

Malachowsky has played a key part in developing the company's computing technology.

Ian Buck, who oversees Nvidia's high-performance computing business, has also emerged as a potential candidate if the company is keen to put an engineer in the top job.

Buck was among the early pioneers who helped popularise the use of graphic processors beyond gaming applications.

"He could protect Nvidia's technological edge, but his ability to manage the wider commercial relationships and geopolitical challenges would face scrutiny," said Charu Chanana, investment strategist at investment bank Saxo.

Today, Nvidia's leading processors are widely regarded as the gold standard for AI development.

Huang's children also work at Nvidia in executive positions but are relatively young and inexperienced.

Nvidia is likely to favour an internal appointment initially but could look externally for a more permanent replacement, said Jun Bei Liu, founder and lead portfolio manager at Ten Cap.

Several names of tech entrepreneurs outside of Nvidia have been floated by experts.

Liu said Apple's former chief executive, Tim Cook, could be a possible choice as someone familiar with managing global supply chains, though she questioned whether he would be willing to leave the iPhone maker, where he is the chair.

Apple's Tim Cook (R) with Jensen Huang (L) and his wife Lori Huang at the White House

Liu and Chanana also suggested Rene Haas, chief executive of Arm Holdings, the UK chipmaking giant.

Nvidia's processors are made with the help of chipmakers around the world, including Taiwan's TSMC and South Korea's SK Hynix and Samsung.

Haas is familiar with leading a publicly-listed company and would bring semiconductor experience, though Nvidia's business model is considerably different from Arm's, said Chanana.

"The challenge is finding someone who can combine Jensen's technical understanding, strategic vision and relationships across the global technology industry," Liu said.

Ten Cap's Liu had believed it was just a matter of time before Huang would step down as Nvidia's chief executive.

"The issue is no longer simply about personal conduct," she said. "It is about corporate governance, national security concerns and maintaining confidence in the leadership of one of the world's most strategically important companies."

Several major investment firms have downgraded their forecasts for the chip giant - a rare shift given investors' longstanding optimism about the company.

An early indication came as markets opened in New York shortly after Huang's resignation was announced.

Nvidia shares opened down more than 13%, wiping more than half a trillion dollars off the firm's share market value.

Jensen Huang's departure leaves a void in the influential AI giant

Stacy Rasgon, senior analyst in US semiconductors at Bernstein Research, wrote in a note to investors that "our gut tells us we probably haven't fully probed the depths of what might be found here".

Rasgon halved Bernstein's share price target for Nvidia and said: "We've had enough and prefer to watch from the sidelines."

Ray Wang, chief executive of Constellation Research, said the crisis isn't so much an issue for the wider AI industry but rather one for Nvidia itself which has to consider if the person it appoints as its chief executive has the same appeal as Huang.

"That's really what the issue is, because a lot of these partnerships and investments have been made because of the trust with with Jensen," he said.

#124. ‘We will sue them’: Canada threatens Stellantis as Trump trade war reshapes Jeep production plans

Canada has threatened to sue auto giant Stellantis over the planned sale of an idled assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario, alleging breaches of a federal funding agreement after the carmaker announced plans to shift production to the U.S. amid President Donald Trump's trade war.

Speaking at a news conference on Thursday, Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said the government had served Stellantis with a formal notice of default, giving the company 30 days to address its concerns or face legal action.

"We sent a notice of default and, basically, they have 30 days — or else, of course, we will sue them," Joly told reporters in Toronto.

Joly said Stellantis had violated its contractual commitments to the Canadian government and the workers at Brampton by announcing an agreement to sell the plant to armored vehicle manufacturer Roshel earlier in the week. She added that the company was now "on the hook" for hundreds of millions of dollars over the move.

Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described the situation at the Brampton plant as "unacceptable" and a "betrayal" of its workers.

The standoff comes as the U.S. and Canada remain locked in a bitter trade dispute, with Trump repeatedly putting pressure on automakers to move vehicle production south of the border.

Stellantis, which owns auto brands including Dodge, Fiat, Chrysler and Peugeot, had scrapped plans to build its Jeep Compass at the Brampton plant and shifted future production to a factory in Illinois amid U.S. auto tariffs and other market pressures.

Stellantis Canada CEO Trevor Longley said in a statement shared with CNBC that the company would return $24 million it received in government support for the Brampton Assembly's original retooling plan. This amount, however, falls significantly short of the Canadian government's own estimate of what the company owes.

"We recognize the disappointment and uncertainty surrounding the future of the facility. While our decision to sell the plant represents a change from the original plans for Brampton, the agreement we have advanced with a Canadian manufacturer provides a path to return the facility to productive industrial use," Longley said.

"As Canada's largest automotive employer, this decision also allows Stellantis to focus its resources on sustaining competitive manufacturing operations in Canada in the face of challenging market realities," he added.

Stellantis said Monday that it had agreed to sell the Brampton assembly plant to Roshel for an undisclosed amount.

It also acknowledged its obligations to employees at the facility and said it remains prepared to work with Unifor, Canada's largest private sector union, to negotiate a settlement to assist union-represented employees.

Stellantis stock has fallen sharply since the start of the year, with its Milan-listed shares down more than 60% for 2026 so far.

— CNBC's Michael Wayland contributed to this report.

#122. Burnham says 'win-win' deal can be agreed between UK and EU

Andy Burnham said he believes a "win-win" deal can be agreed with the European Union, adding it could be "more like the Brexit that people thought they were voting for" in 2016.

The prime minister is exploring closer ties with the bloc and is expected to sign new agreements on food, energy and youth mobility at a UK-EU summit on 20 November.

Speaking after meeting European leaders in Ireland on Thursday, Burnham said he hopes a deal could help lower prices and boost the UK economy. He predicted the negotiations could "go down to the wire".

The Conservatives have previously said Labour are "dragging us into the European Union via the back door with their EU reset".

While Burnham has argued that Brexit has done "more harm than good", he has ruled out major changes to the UK's relationship with the EU before the next general election.

Labour's 2024 general election manifesto pledged there would be "no return to the single market, the customs union, or freedom of movement", and Burnham has committed to this.

The manifesto also said the party wanted to "make Brexit work" and reset the UK's relationship with the EU.

Former Prime Minister Sir Keir Starmer struck a deal in May 2025 covering fishing, trade and other areas and next week's summit will see the UK government seek to agree further changes.

Burnham has also made clear he wants to examine the "pros and cons" of the options for Britain's long-term relationship with the bloc.

Burnham's push for closer EU ties causes ripples in his party and others

UK cannot 'cherry pick' EU single market deal, Barnier tells BBC

Brexit referendum 'possible' in Labour election manifesto, says Burnham

Can Burnham avoid another Labour welfare rebellion?

Speaking to reporters on Thursday, Burnham was asked to explain if his decision to reopen the debate on the UK's future with Europe had helped or hindered the negotiations linked to the summit.

Burnham replied "I think it has helped" before adding: "We've got a big week ahead of us to try and get this deal over the line.

"But let's be clear what's at stake: what we can achieve through this deal is lower prices for British consumers, lower costs for British businesses, more growth in our economy so there's really big prizes here, and I will fight now over the days to come to make sure we get this deal over the line."

Pressed on whether discounted university tuition fees for European students would be part of the deal, Burnham said "requests are coming from the European side, we are putting our requests on the table too".

He added: "I believe there is a good deal to be done that could be a win-win for Britain and Europe. I think it actually could be more like the Brexit that people thought they were voting for in 2016."

Ahead of the summit, the UK and EU agreed a draft deal on removing post-Brexit checks from food and agriculture exports.

Similar texts for deals on emissions trading and a youth travel and work scheme have also been circulated to EU nations.

As a result of the agreement, the government claims the UK economy will eventually see Â£5.1bn worth of annual benefits.

Burnham also told MPs last month that the summit presents an opportunity to make progress on "controlling illegal immigration".

Conservative Party leader Kemi Badenoch has previously strongly opposed any move to align with EU rules, saying it would "actually take us backwards".

On Thursday, Conservative MP and former minister Dame Priti Patel said: "Andy Burnham's obsession with the EU risks dragging Britain backwards, damaging our economy and leaving hardworking British taxpayers footing the bill."

The Liberal Democrats earlier this month described the draft deal as a "patch, not a fix" and said the "real solution is to re-join the single market and to establish a customs union with the EU".

Reform UK believes one of the "key benefits" of Brexit is that Britain no longer has to "adhere to ridiculous EU rules" and any form of alignment could have a negative impact on the country's trade policy.

The Green Party has called for the UK to re-join the customs union, with leader Zack Polanski previously saying "full membership of the EU remains the best option for the UK".

#121. CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations 2026: How the 32 franchises stack up

For the first time, hockey team valuations have crossed the $4 billion threshold, with CNBC's NHL valuations led by the Toronto Maple Leafs, at $5.4 billion, the New York Rangers, at $4.7 billion, and the Montreal Canadiens, at $4.1 billion. The average NHL team is now worth $2.71 billion, 23% more than a year ago.

Don't be fooled by the lone NHL control sale to close over the past 12 months: the $1.8 billion sale of the Pittsburgh Penguins to the Hoffmann family. Although the transaction was approved by the league in June, the price was negotiated about 18 months ago.

Look instead at the latest minority stake transactions. In March the NHL approved the sale of a 12.5% stake in the Carolina Hurricanes by Tom Dundon in a transaction that valued the team at $2.66 billion. Scott Malkin and other shareholders are close to selling a 15% stake in the New York Islanders at a $3 billion valuation. Sports bankers tell CNBC these deals are representative of what control stakes in the two teams would sell for given the hot market for sports teams.

"Like the other major sports leagues there is a strong demand to own hockey teams because their media content values are high and there is a scarcity of teams," said Sal Galatioto, whose firm, Galatioto Sports Partners, advises clients on both the buy and sell sides of sports team transactions. "Capitalism and technology are creating more wealth and the NHL, like the NFL, NBA and MLB, is an industry with a very long product life cycle, a hedge against risk in AI."

The NHL's current national media rights deals with Walt Disney and Warner Bros. Discovery are worth a combined annual average of close to $650 million through the 2027-28 season, but given the league's strong television ratings last season, a big increase could be in the offing.

"The 2025-26 NHL regular season averaged 546,000 viewers across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports, up 25% from last year and the highest average in more than a decade," Sports Media Watch wrote. "The 25% increase is on the high end of a general rise in sports viewership that includes gains of 35% for the NBA, 19% for men's college basketball and 10% for the NFL."

Sports Media Watch also wrote that the 2026 NHL Stanley Cup Playoffs averaged 1.8 million viewers in the U.S. across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports. That's up 68% from 2025, and it's the highest average on record for the playoffs on American television, going back to 1994, it said.

The NHL is pondering adding teams in cities such as Houston and Atlanta, and another factor fueling NHL valuations is the belief that the expansion fees paid by the new teams would surpass $2 billion. The Seattle Kraken, the NHL's most recent expansion team, paid a $650 million fee to join the league for the 2021-22 season.

According to CNBC's figures, revenue for the league's 32 teams averaged $257 million for the 2025-26 season, 6% more than the previous year, while EBITDA — earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization — averaged $48 million per team, a decline of 11% due to arena expenses. The league's profitability should rebound as money spent on hospitality, concessions and merchandise stores boosts revenue.

In April 2025 the NHL and Rogers Communications agreed to a 12-year national Canadian media rights deal worth $7.79 billion, based on exchange rates as of Nov. 24, that will begin with the 2026-27 season. That's more than twice the amount the league is getting from its existing 12-year deal with Rogers. This will help push up the league's hockey-related revenue — which includes media rights, sponsorships, premium seating, tickets, concessions and parking — to around $7.25 billion for the 2026-27 season, 7% more than last season.

CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations are current enterprise values — equity plus net debt — calculated using revenue multiples, and include the economics of the team's arena, including non-NHL revenue that accrues to the team's owner. The valuations are meant to represent what a control stake valuation would go for in an arm's-length transaction and do not include the value of the arena or the real estate it sits on.

Revenue figures are net of revenue sharing and the 35% of home playoff non-premium gate revenue that goes toward the league's revenue-sharing pool. Revenue multiples are based on historical transaction prices, as well as what investors, league executives and bankers say about the current market.

CNBC values exclude other businesses related to the teams with separate financial statements, such as the Edmonton Oilers' mixed-use real estate project. For teams that sell their own merchandise, such as the Oilers, CNBC deducts the cost of goods sold from merchandise sales. Likewise, CNBC excludes the market value of affiliates/equity method investments to ensure consistent enterprise value-to-revenue and enterprise value-to-EBITDA multiples. Debt figures are the latest available and include both team debt and stadium debt.

CNBC incorporates the value of a pending new arena in our valuations if construction has begun and financing has been secured. For example, the $2.25 billion valuation for the Calgary Flames incorporates their new arena, which is scheduled to open in time for the 2027-28 season. Likewise, CNBC valuations anticipate a substantial increase in the NHL's next U.S. national broadcasting deal, which would begin with the 2028-29 season.

Revenue and EBITDA figures are for the 2025-26 season and net of revenue sharing and player escrow. CNBC presents revenue and operating income figures on a cash basis, rather than an accrual basis. CNBC figures are different from the league's official hockey-related revenue because we include non-NHL arena revenue that goes to the teams. When the owner of the arena also owns both the NHL team and NBA team that play in the same building — as is the case for several teams, including Toronto's Maple Leafs and Raptors, who are both owned by Rogers Communications and play in Scotiabank Arena — we divide revenue from non-sports events held at the arena evenly between the two teams.

All valuation figures are in U.S. dollars. The exchange rate for revenue and EBITDA is based on a one-year average, from June 2025 to June 2026, of CA$1 = US$0.72, and the exchange rate for team values and debt, as of Sept. 21, is CA$1 = US$0.71.

Sources for CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations include team owners, investors and executives, as well as sports bankers and league consultants; public documents such as stadium lease agreements, stadium authority budget audits and credit rating reports; and sponsorship and broadcasting industry executives. Certain figures were provided by league executives who spoke to CNBC on the condition of anonymity because agreements are private and they do not have permission to speak about them publicly. Figures that could not be confirmed with sources are CNBC estimates.

— CNBC's Jungwoo Ethan Kim contributed to this report.

#119. Heat pumps: How much heat does your home really need?

Floorplans. Air duct diagrams. Heat pump specifications. Most people in the market for a new heating system probably don't want to think about this level of detail.

But, perhaps surprisingly, some do want to think about those things. In extreme depth.

Every day, roughly 150 new users sign up to an app called Cool Calc, which works out the heat requirements of properties in the US.

Just over one third of those new users are not tradespeople but private homeowners, says founder and chief executive Bill Fiore.

When choosing a heating system, people can find themselves bombarded with information â how powerful a heat pump do they need, for example? It isn't always easy to take a tradesperson at their word.

"As an overall trend, we are seeing homeowners be more informed," explains Fiore. "They want to do a calculation on their own."

Cool Calc is used by tradespeople and homeowners to work out heat loss

A heat loss calculation is a figure, usually measured in watts, that defines how much heat a home loses during cold weather. It's worked out room by room and is a key part of designing an efficient heating system.

This estimate is especially important to get right when installing a heat pump, which is sized fairly precisely, according to the projected heat loss.

Should that calculation be off, the heat pump will likely perform poorly. You could be left with an underpowered device that struggles to warm your property, for example. Given the costs involved, homeowners are often anxious to ensure installers avoid such mistakes.

Certain new technologies are not, for the moment, helping, adds Fiore. "People are going to AI and they're getting a lot of misinformation because it's not backed by anything credible," he says. Cool Calc is planning to integrate with AI chatbots to try and improve the quality of the advice they spew out.

It is probably a good idea to be cautious about your new heating system's design, says Kevin Kircher at Purdue University. He and colleagues recently conducted a study, yet to be published, examining the heat loss calculations provided by tradespeople to roughly 75 US homeowners.

"We were surprised," says Kircher. "The heat loss calculations often led to overestimating the design heat loads by a factor of two or more." That would result in oversized systems that use much more power than necessary.

Not only that, some people reported charges of more than $1,000 (Â£745) â and in one case as much as $2,500 â just for a heat loss survey, adds Kircher.

Besides using excessive amounts of energy, an oversized heating system can also feel uncomfortable, says Dan Dempsey, a former senior engineering fellow for a major heating and cooling provider in the US.

Dempsey, who took part in Kircher and his colleagues' study, lives in Indiana. He says it is his retirement hobby to monitor the performance of heating and cooling systems in the homes of friends and family members, by analysing data from their smart thermostats.

"I spent five years manually downloading everything, every week," he says. He points out that oversized systems tend to cycle a lot, meaning they switch on and off again in quick succession.

This leads to discernible swings in temperature: rooms that fail to remain at their target temperature of, say, 20C, but rather fluctuate, which feels uncomfortable, says Dempsey.

For people seeking a better understanding of their home's heat loss, various free or partially free apps, including Cool Calc and AutoHVAC, are available. There are options for UK homeowners, too, such as web app Heatpunk.

Heat loss apps help drive business to renewable energy firms says Andy Rankin

Many things can influence the heat loss figure, including the amount of insulation in your home, whether the building has double or triple glazing, and also the size of your radiators.

Heatpunk allows people to draw a floor plan of their home and input data about the property in order to estimate its performance. Thousands of people use it every month, mostly professional installers, says Andy Rankin, founder and managing director of Midsummer Energy.

The web app also suggests specific heat pump models for each property, based on the results of the calculation. Naturally, these are models available through Midsummer Energy. "It's been a very effective marketing tool," says Rankin.

Dwellow, another company that offers heat pump installations in the UK, is keen to follow suit. The firm recently launched its own heat loss estimator.

While professional heat loss calculations remain essential, getting people to do their own "self surveys" first could ultimately reduce how much time professional heating engineers must spend analysing properties says Ian Mclaren, Dwellow's co-founder and director.

"Going into a home and just being able to check the information rather than start from scratch could turn a survey into a half-hour trip â far more efficient and less costly," says Mclaren.

There's no substitute for engineers doing a good job when it is their turn, however, stresses Maurizio Sibilla, of the Centre for Advanced Built Environment Research at the University of West England, Bristol.

"The solution is better installer practice, clearer customer-facing evidence, and improved methods, not expecting homeowners to do the engineering themselves," he says, adding that providing homeowners with information about the calculation and also data on heat pump performance post-installation can reassure them that the system works as expected.

Adam Chapman questions whether homeowners should do their own heat loss survey

Doing your own heat loss calculation may end up "over-intellectualising" things, says Adam Chapman, chief geek at heat pump training firm and installer network Heat Geek.

"I don't personally see what a homeowner would gain from it," he says. "We will guarantee the efficiency with way less radiators and pipework upgrades than any other system out there."

Chapman points out that only the most careful of surveys will capture a home's heat loss well.

He says it is common for Heat Geek installers to discover retrofitted windows that haven't been foam-insulated into their openings, leaving draughty gaps, or cavity walls that a homeowner thought had been insulated â but which were actually missed by the builder.

"It's stuff you can't see," he says. "What we actually need is home surveys that go and spot for these sorts of things."

Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

'An annoying buzz': The search for stealthier drones

The Indian workers filming their work to train AI

#118. U.S. seizes $1.2 billion in cryptocurrency stolen from Binance

U.S. authorities have seized around $1.2 billion in virtual assets stolen from cryptocurrency exchange Binance last month, the Department of Justice said Thursday, as it vowed action to tackle cyberscams originating from North Korea.

Over $6 billion was stolen from industry giant Binance on Oct. 29, which the DOJ attributed to actors linked to the North Korean military.

Collective efforts between the U.S. government and private sector have resulted in more than $2.7 billion of that amount being frozen.

The FBI blames the attack, which shook confidence in crypto market security, on members of the North Korean military it refers to as "TraderTraitor."

The DOJ said it was taking coordinated action against what it called the North Korean government's "schemes to fund its regime through the massive theft of cryptocurrency."

That includes "the court-authorized seizure and restraint of more than $1.2 billion worth of cryptocurrency recently stolen from Binance," it said.

DOJ units, law enforcement partners and virtual asset service providers have been working to intercept the stolen assets.

But efforts to track and freeze the assets are ongoing, as TraderTraitor actors continue to launder them through various cryptocurrency channels.

Authorities have also seized cloud-based infrastructure used in a massive identity impersonation scam known as "FaceStealer," which has seen more than $400 million worth of cryptocurrency stolen over 18 months, the DOJ said.

The scheme sees hackers set up fake video meetings using stolen accounts and recordings of people known to the target by using AI-generated deepfakes. The hackers then say a technical problem requires a fix, install malware on a computer and take over the victim's communications, steal their cryptocurrency and target their contacts — sometimes months or more than a year after the initial compromise.

Individuals working in the cryptocurrency and web3 industries are prime targets.

Assistant Director in Charge Patrick Grandy, of the FBI's Los Angeles Field Office, called FaceStealer "a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user."

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships. If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough," Grandy added.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft," Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor said in a statement.

"America will fight back against that corrupt regime's effort to profit through cybercrime. The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea's ill-gotten gains."

#116. Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant here, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12 to 18 months "would be great" and is something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said plant construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an internal combustion engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said extended-range and SUV models are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla.

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start at under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exteriors that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

#115. Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has been summoned to testify next month before a Senate committee about his alleged affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Huang abruptly quit on Thursday as head of one of the world's most valuable companies after revelations of alleged extramarital affairs, in a stunning fall from grace that sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who is under scrutiny over exports of sensitive Nvidia AI chips to China, has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December.

Nvidia has lost $550bn (Â£415bn) in stock market value in just five days as the scandal dominated headlines.

Senator Tim Scott sent letters on Thursday to Huang and Lutnick requesting their testimony in front of the Senate Banking Committee.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," the South Carolina Republican posted on X.

"The American people deserve answers," he added.

Scott asked Huang to appear voluntarily on 3 December, but also warned the panel could issue a subpoena to compel his testimony.

Three House of Representatives committees - on intelligence and foreign affairs and a panel on China - have also sent a letter to Huang summoning him for a closed-door interview on 1 December.

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

Lutnick is under scrutiny because unnamed officials in President Donald Trump's administration have been briefing US media that the commerce secretary stalled efforts to tighten export restrictions on advanced AI chips to China.

A commerce department spokesperson told US media that Lutnick had "proactively" agreed to appear before the Senate Banking Committee.

Huang, who has been Nvidia's public face since 1993, admitted on Sunday to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang.

On Wednesday, he was reported to have had a second relationship, this time with an Nvidia executive.

Alice Huang (no relation) worked as an executive at the Singaporean firm Megaspeed. She has been suspected of being an intelligence asset for China, according to the New York Times. China has rejected that allegation as a smear.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December

US Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday the Department of Justice had not yet drawn any conclusions about whether Alice Huang was engaged in espionage for Beijing.

Nvidia officials have repeatedly declined to comment since Wednesday. A personal representative of Huang also declined to comment on Thursday.

According to US media, Huang's alleged second affair was with a long-time Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reported directly to him within the company.

Sources have told the BBC that Nvidia employees had noticed the pair were unusually close.

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Huang had been widely expected to weather the storm after the first affair became public, even as Nvidia announced it had launched an investigation into whether its CEO had been "the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

One current Nvidia staff member told the BBC that the initial reports of his infidelity were more or less brushed off by rank-and-file workers inside the company.

It wasn't until Huang's alleged affair with the Nvidia executive became public that workers started to feel "this is crazy", the person said.

Another Nvidia employee said they had been told not to speak with the press or otherwise comment on the situation in public.

Many staff have earned millions of dollars in compensation through their ability to cash out their Nvidia shares as the stock soared.

Nvidia has said it has "no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised", but pledged that its independent inquiry would look at all available information.

The company's meteoric rise had been led by Jensen Huang, who became a major booster of the AI industry, which relies on Nvidia for the powerful computer chips it manufactures.

Initially those chips were designed for computer gaming.

But as AI technology evolved in the last four years into chatbots and large language models, companies building AI tools suddenly required huge numbers of the Nvidia computer chips that could process large amounts of data.

Nvidia is now hunting a permanent replacement for Huang. Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress was named on Thursday as interim CEO.

Several tech insiders that the BBC spoke with this week noted that Huang's downfall has been a major topic of conversation in San Francisco Bay area circles, an area filled with tech employees, industry executives and billionaires.

Huang has been married to Lori Huang for 40 years. Both of their children are also executives at Nvidia.

Nvidia's stock fall this week is the most significant five-day drop the company has seen since going public 27 years ago.

Its market value rose by more than 470% in the past five years, peaking just a few weeks ago. It is now down by 31% against a backdrop of wider uncertainty in the tech sector.

#114. Trump says he wants to ‘get Cuba done’ before leaving office, would consider military force

U.S. President Donald Trump has said he wants to "get Cuba done" before leaving office in 2029, adding that he would "certainly consider" military intervention against the communist-run Caribbean island.

In an interview with conservative radio host Hugh Hewitt on Thursday, Trump said his administration was monitoring the situation in Havana "very closely" and described Cuba as a "failed" state.

"I want to get Cuba done before I leave," Trump said. His comments marked the first time he has put a timeline on possible military intervention to topple Cuban authorities, according to Politico.

Trump said Secretary of State Marco Rubio, whose parents are from Cuba, "would love to do it" and said he would "certainly consider" a short military operation.

It comes as the U.S. continues to enforce a comprehensive economic embargo against Cuba, a campaign which has contributed to a deepening energy and economic crisis on the island. Cuba has suffered repeated nationwide blackouts in recent months, as well as fuel and food shortages.

The Cuban Embassy in Washington, D.C., estimated on Thursday that the cost of the U.S. blockade to Cuba between March 2025 and February 2026 amounted to $8 billion.

Earlier this month, the Treasury Department imposed further sanctions on Cuban oil tankers and shipping companies and threatened to target any international entity or person doing business within its marine or maritime industries as part of a push "to end the Cuban regime's malign activities, both in Cuba and across our hemisphere."

In response, Cuba's Embassy in Washington, D.C., said these steps "deepen an energy siege that already leaves hospitals without reliable power and families facing blackouts. The blockade is not targeted at a government. It is collective punishment that hits ordinary people every day."

Trump has previously talked up the prospect of a "friendly takeover" of Havana and said the White House could turn its sights on Cuba after the Iran war. The U.S. president has also said he could do anything he wanted with the country, adding that he thinks he will have the "honor" of "taking Cuba."

In May, Cuban Energy Minister Vicente de la O Levy said the country of roughly 10 million had run out of fuel oil and diesel, describing the country's predicament as "extremely tense."

Analysts have typically been skeptical about the prospect of a U.S. military campaign against Cuba, suggesting that further economic pressure against the island's authorities appears more likely.

#112. The rise of 'housefishing' - when does using AI go from enhancing to misleading?

The images Olivia Nicole saw of a home on Rightmove (l) were completely different to how the property looked in real life (r)

Have you ever been impressed by the pictures of a property on an estate agent's website, only to find the place looks completely different when you view it in person?

Then there's a chance that AI was used to enhance the images you saw.

From repainted walls to imaginary lawns, estate agents are increasingly using the technology to make homes seem more appealing, arguing it helps buyers visualise the potential of a place.

But experts warn it can go too far and mislead prospective buyers - a trend dubbed "housefishing".

Earlier this year Olivia Nicole, 29, was searching online for five-bedroom properties in south-west London when a house on Rightmove caught her eye.

But when she arrived to view it several days later she initially thought she had come to the wrong place.

"The rooms were much smaller than they were in the pictures and the decor was totally different," she tells the BBC. "Some structural features were also different."

For example, there was a raised section of the wall behind the sofa that didn't appear in the images. The windows were also noticeably smaller. Olivia was so surprised she took her own pictures of the property for comparison.

AI has been used to make the property look more luxurious

It later turned out the pictures she had seen on Rightmove, which also appeared on the Zoopla and Winkworth websites, were enhanced with AI.

But that wasn't clear in the listing, Olivia says, and she feels the agent wasted her time.

"It was frustrating, because I had taken several hours off work for the viewing which I had to make up for."

All three companies told the BBC they had strict guidelines on how AI could be used on their websites and have since taken the images down.

Estate agents have long used wide-angle lens cameras or Photoshop to make homes seem bigger or more appealing to buyers, but AI is making this process much easier and more accessible.

It is difficult to quantify how common the practice is, says the Guild of Property Professionals, a trade group of independent estate agents, but it has issued guidance on how to use AI enhancement responsibly.

According to a recent survey, external of 906 UK homebuyers, one in five who had who purchased a home in the past year said they had viewed a property where the listing photos had been digitally enhanced to make the home appear better than it actually was.

Jack Bushrod reviews quirky property listings he finds on Rightmove for his social media channel, Housing Horrors, and says he has seen a "surge" of AI-enhanced images over the last six months.

He thinks using AI is fine up to a point - to declutter a busy room, or add furniture to an empty one so a prospective buyer can see its potential.

But he draws the line at anything that misleads a prospective buyer or wastes their time.

"I've seen one house where they had removed a pillar and added a window. Others might use AI to remove stains on the carpet or cracks in tiles. It's little details you would only notice in person."

He says most of the enhanced images he's seen weren't clearly labelled as AI-generated.

Cara says AI was used to empty her son's room in the image on the left

Cara, 38, is currently selling her home in Lowestoft, Suffolk, and says she was surprised when she saw the AI-enhanced pictures of her property her estate agent, Howards, put on Rightmove.

In one photo, her son's room was completely emptied of toys and clutter.

In another, of her daughter's room, the agent appears to have removed a doll's house, brightened the room, and used AI to change the shape of a rug, but that image has since been taken down.

Cara has had several viewings so far and says that one person has commented on the differences. But she says she trusts Howards, which has acknowledged the use of AI in the property listing.

"If they'd changed something structurally or it was really wild and didn't look like our home at all then I would put my foot down," she says.

In one picture (l), the AI removed a doll's house and changed the shape of a rug

A spokeswoman told the BBC that Howards had now introduced a policy of labelling all AI-enhanced photos. She added that the firm never used AI "to misrepresent the fundamental features of a property".

"It's about helping people imagine the possibilities, not changing the reality of what's being sold."

Trading Standards told the BBC that estate agents can use AI to make basic enhancements to photos, or to virtually furnish an empty room or garden, as long as they indicate they have used the technology.

But it says structurally changing a room, for example by adding a window, or hiding features could mislead consumers and break the law.

Jack Bushrod thinks all estate agents should have to clearly label AI-enhanced photos and mention any use of the tech prominently in the key features slide of a property listing.

He also expects to see more housefishing as time goes on.

"My worry is the more estate agents use AI, others will see the trend and jump on the bandwagon."
